===PAGE 3=== (ocr, 24 lines)
   1|                                                                                 In Memoriam
   2|                   Emily M. (Moore) Foster, 96, a longtime Lunenburg resident, passed away peacefully on June 28, 2021.
   3|                 From Cub Scout Den Mother to beloved Children's Librarian to Meals on Wheels volunteer to busy Senior
   4|                 Center member, Emily was active in the Lunenburg community, and enjoyed golf and bowling leagues and
   5|                  the "Bookworms" club. Emily loved her "Story Hour" kids from her 20+ years as the Children's Librarian
   6|                       of the Ritter Memorial Library in Lunenburg. After retirement, she became an active member of the
   7|                 Lunenburg Friendly Seniors and the Eagle House Senior Center. She also worked for years as a poll worker
   8|                            for elections in Lunenburg and assisted in the yard sales for the United Parish of Lunenburg.
   9|                                                                 Robert M. Herrick, "Mickey" Herrick. 73, passed away on May 9th,
  10|                                                                 2021, surrounded by his immediate family. He was a graduate of
  11|                                                                 Lunenburg High School during which time he was Vice President of his
  12|                                                                 class and an accomplished athlete. Mickey continued his love of sports
  13|                                                                 having played men's softball on numerous local teams throughout his life.
  14|                                                                 He truly cherished the lifelong friendships and memories that were made
  15|                                                                 while he was playing. Mickey was a prominent figure in the community
  16|                                                                 and a local business owner for over 45 years. He was most known for
  17|                                                                 "Mickey's Place,", a Lunenburg staple which served wonderful food and
  18|                                                                 spirits for over 28 years. When not cracking jokes across the bar, Mickey
  19|                                                                 could be found coaching T-ball, Little League and LHS Varsity Softball,
  20|                                                                 and serving on the Trustee Board over the years. He was a passionate
  21|                 sports fan and loved spending his free time falling asleep in his chair watching a Boston Red Sox or New
  22|                 England Patriots game, Howard Stern, or anything political. More than anything, Mickey loved to spend
  23|                 time with his wife, Kathleen, children and grandchildren.
  24|                                                                                                2

===PAGE 6=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                                                          LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                       YEAR INCORPORATED—1728
   3|                                                                        REGISTERED VOTERS- 8635
   4|                                                                           FY'21 TAX RATE- $17.74
   5|                                               OPEN TOWN GOVERNMENT - 5 MEMBER SELECT BOARD
   6|                                                  ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                                    ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                                                        AREA-26.63 SQUARE MILES
   9|                                                                          ROAD MILES—ABOUT 100
  10|                Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4
  11|                 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts approximately 43 miles from Boston. It
  12|                is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of
  13|                 Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by the Town of
  14|                 Townsend.
  15|                 Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters in the town.
  16|                 Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are generally administered
  17|                 by a five member Select Board and a Town Manager. Local school affairs are administered by an elected
  18|                 seven member School Committee while local taxes are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all
  19|                 are appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms.
  20|                 Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies account
  21|                 for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development. Lake Whalom and the former
  22|                 Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This district
  23|                 was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster
  24|                 and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect characteristics of
  25|                 these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  26|                 developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The third body of water is
  27|                 Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been
  28|                 modified to year-round residences.
  29|                 Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major
  30|                 arteries (Routes 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's
  31|                 and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into
  32|                 single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg. In 2013 the Annual Town Meeting voted to
  33|                 establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve and enhance farming in the community and to
  34|                 administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw.
  35|                 The 2015 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District") bylaw to
  36|                 regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" structures as defined by
  37|                 the bylaw within an established bounded district. The bylaw was introduced at Town Meeting along with
  38|                 the Village District Bylaw, which created an overlay zoning district to encourage mixed-use commercial and
  39|                 residential development. While the Village District Bylaw encourages the economic vitalization of
  40|                 downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District Bylaw ensures that it will not come at the
  41|                 expense of the town's historical character and integrity.
  42|                                                                                                5

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                 REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                                                                                FISCAL YEAR 2021
   3|               Like many others, there was hope going into fiscal year 2021 that COVID-19 would start to become a thing of
   4|                the past. However, after over a year into the pandemic, it was still a challenge that we had to surmount while
   5|               providing services to our residents and simultaneously keeping everyone safe. There wasn't a department that
   6|                was immune to COVID- whether it was being a "close contact", having to quarantine or isolate became a
   7|               regular occurrence.
   8|                Despite the lingering challenge that COVID-19 continued to pose in continuity of services, fiscal year 2021
   9|               was a year of progress. Below I would like to highlight the topics that were addressed and the
  10|               accomplishments within the Office of the Town Manager:
  11|               • In July of 2020, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed the Town Manager's
  12|                     goals from FY20 and voted to accept the Town Manager's Goals for FY21. The Town Manager's goals
  13|                     for FY 2021 included continuing to take the necessary steps towards the redevelopment/reuse of 925
  14|                     Massachusetts Avenue, looking at the feasibility of creating an independent facilities department and
  15|                     improve the long term maintenance and repair plan for town facilities, and succession planning. Progress
  16|                     was made on different action items and two of the goals have carried forward into FY 2022.
  17|               • In regards to 925 Massachusetts Avenue, the Town Manager continued to work with the BETA Group on
  18|                     environmental site assessments for that property and with MassDevelopment on any possible funding
  19|                     available to clean-up the property. During FY 2021 the Phase II environmental site assessment was
  20|                     completed and the Town Manager requested additional funding for a Phase III environmental site
  21|                     assessment that would identify the costs of cleanup of the identified hazardous materials and removal of
  22|                     underground tanks. The Town Manager also applied for a Brownfields Redevelopment Fund grant for
  23|                      $250,000 in September of 2020, but unfortunately this property did not yet qualify for that funding. The
  24|                      Town Manager also worked with the DPW Director to hire a surveyor to do survey the property.
  25|                • As it related to one of the action items under the Town Manager's Annual Goals for Facilities, in April the
  26|                      Town Manager announced that a new Facilities Management software called eMaint would be utilized for
  27|                      facility work orders, scheduling preventative maintenance, and overall asset management. This was paid
  28|                      for by a grant through our insurance carrier.
  29|                • The Select Board finalized their Goals for FY 2021 in September of 2020. One of the Select Board goals
  30|                      for FY 2021 was to identify, define and propose at least one project to the 2021 Annual Town Meeting
  31|                     regarding the use and/or re-use of the Old Primary School building, and/or the land on which it sits. The
  32|                      Board held a site visit to the property at the end of October 2020 to review the property and boundaries. In
  33|                      January the Board met with the Architectural Preservation District Commission and the Historical
  34|                      Commission about the Select Board plans to see happen with that property and how to navigate a path
  35|                      forward. The Town Manager and the Select Board worked with Monty Tech on conceptual drawings to
  36|                      demolish the existing old Primary School building and create an a passive recreation/open space area for
  37|                      the Farmers Market, band concerts and other community events. At the May 2021 Annual Town Meeting
  38|                      the Town approved funding to hire a Landscape Architect to prepare conceptual plans for this site and a
  39|                      cost estimate for the overall project.
  40|                 • Another one of the Select Board goals for FY 2021 was to review, discuss and revise (where needed) the
  41|                      re-appointment process of all annual Select Board appointments. In February the Board held a workshop
  42|                      to formalize their process into an appointment policy. Ultimately the Board mostly deferred to the original
  43|                      Annual Select Board Appointment Process but defined the timeline and notifying chairs of the committees
  44|                      of persons wishing to be appointed/reappointed.
  45|                                                                                               17

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                • Fiscal Year 2021 was also a year for retirements for two long serving town employees, Police Chief
   2|                     James Marino and DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz. Chief Marino retired on July 31st after working for
   3|                     the Town of Lunenburg for over forty years. On October 30th, Jack Rodriquenz retired after over twenty
   4|                      five years working for the Town. The Town was fortunate to have both Jim and Jack leading the Police
   5|                      and DPW Departments, respectively and wish them all the best in their retirement. Lieutenant Thomas
   6|                      Gammel was appointed the Acting Police Chief in August and after conducting a search to fill the
   7|                      position, was selected to fill the position permanently in October. Similarly, the DPW Director position
   8|                      was posted in September and after conducting a search the Town Manager appointed Robert Oliva to fill
   9|                     the position at the beginning of November.
  10|                • Also in July the Select Board met with Sean O'Neill of Bright Farms (MA Greenhouse LLC) relative to a
  11|                      proposed major greenhouse that they were looking to build on the land behind Walmart on Massachusetts
  12|                      Avenue and their request for a Tax Increment Financing (TIF) Agreement. The proposal included an
  13|                     initial investment of $10 million and the creation of jobs that would be offered first to Lunenburg
  14|                      residents. After negotiating with Bright Farms over the terms of a TIF agreement, this was voted upon
  15|                      favorably at the November Special Town Meeting. The final TIF provided a 10 year tax discount to the
  16|                      company with a 100% discount in real estate taxes on the new construction in year 1 and decreased by
  17|                      10% each year of the agreement until year 10 where the Town would be receiving 90% of the real estate
  18|                      taxes. In Year 11 Bright Farms would be required to pay the full tax assessment. At the November 2020
  19|                      Special Town Meeting the Town approved the TIF agreement as presented. Ultimately the corporate
  20|                      offices of Bright Farms chose another location that better fit their business model and the project did not
  21|                      move forward.
  22|                • At the beginning of this fiscal year the Town Manager reported that she worked with the Open Space
  23|                      Chairman, the Finance Director, DPW, and Conservation Administrator to ensure that all requirements of
  24|                      the PARC and LAND Grants to purchase the Saliba property were done in order to close on the property
  25|                      in time to submit for reimbursement of these grants. The Town Manager submitted all the required
  26|                      documentation for the LAND and PARC grants, totaling $500,000, to ensure the Town received this
  27|                      reimbursement to purchase this park and open space property.
  28|                • The Town Manager and Select Board conducted an annual review of the Select Board's policies in
  29|                      August and finalized the review in September.
  30|                • This year was another productive year for infrastructure projects in Lunenburg. Besides the continuation
  31|                      of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town began the reconstruction of the Pleasant
  32|                      Street Bridge that was completed in the fall of 2021. As part of paving in the area of Whalom Road, the
  33|                      ADA accessible ramps were incorporated. The Complete Streets design work was finalized during the
  34|                      fiscal year and the construction portion went out to bid in March of 2021. This project wrapped up in the
  35|                      fall of 2021. The Town Manager also worked with the DPW Director on continuing the discussions about
  36|                      the drainage issues on Flat Hill Road and engaging McCarty Engineering to look at drainage solutions.
  37|                      On another section of Flat Hill Road, a culvert project that had received funding in the FY21 Capital Plan
  38|                      had to be delayed due to a revised estimated construction cost for the project due to rising construction
  39|                      and materials cost due to the pandemic that increased the total cost from $428,000 to $750,000. The Town
  40|                      Manager and the DPW Director worked with the engineer to apply for the Small Bridge Grant Program.
  41|                      and seek earmark requests from the State. To date the Town is waiting on the whether the Small Bridge
  42|                      Grant application that was submitted will be approved for funding.
  43|                • Due to another building at Tri-Town being built for persons over 55, the Town received a $210,000
  44|                      Density Bonus Payment per MGL c. 40R (Smart Growth Zoning District) from the Department of
  45|                      Housing and Community Development. These funds would close out at the end of FY21 and positively
  46|                      impact the certified free cash available in FY22.
  47|                                                                                               18

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|               • In November Green Leaf Botanicals met with the Select Board to discuss a proposed Adult Use Retail
   2|                     Marijuana establishment at 88 Massachusetts Avenue. Green Leaf met with the Board again in December
   3|                    to address the timeline for holding a Community Outreach Meeting and negotiating the terms of a Host
   4|                     Community Agreement. Discussions are still occurring in 2021 and a Host Community Agreement was
   5|                    signed in FY22.
   6|               • The Special Town Meeting in November 2020 included a number of articles such as amending the FY
   7|                    2021 budget, voting on the FY21 Capital Plan projects that were deferred at the June 2020 Annual Town
   8|                     Meeting, funding one year Collective Bargaining Agreements for the Fire and Clerical Unions, and
   9|                    appropriating funds to move forward with the purchase of the streetlights from Unitil in order to
  10|                     eventually convert them to LED.
  11|               • After notification from a town resident about a possible dumping site at the Woodruff property (104
  12|                     Fairview Road) at the November 2020 Special Town Meeting, the Town Manager engaged a consultant
  13|                    that performed an Environmental Site Assessment. In May the Town received the results of that
  14|                     assessment that identified the solid waste materials at the site that dated back to the mid-1950's to early
  15|                     1970's when it was a farm and prior to the Town purchasing the property. The assessment recommended
  16|                     a method of removing and disposing of the solid waste materials and an estimated cost of between
  17|                     $72,000 to $84,000. The Town Manager would continue to pursue clean-up at this site and work with the
  18|                     Conservation Commission on an Order of Conditions to do the work well into FY22.
  19|               • In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday
  20|                     entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses. Throughout the year, the
  21|                     Board reviews Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers, , flammable storage licenses, pole
  22|                     petitions, tollbooth requests, First Right of Refusal Requests for Chapter 61, properties, and Dangerous
  23|                     Dog hearings.
  24|               • The Town Manager presented a Five Year Forecast and Capital Plan for the funding of 13 projects
  25|                     totaling $1,499,825 and a 10 year look ahead at projected capital needs.
  26|               • In February the Select Board met with Town Counsel and the Conservation Commission Chairman to
  27|                     discuss carrying firearms on town property and hunting on conservation land. Due to the recent review of
  28|                     the Conservation Commission Land Use Regulations, this topic needed to be addressed due to some areas
  29|                     of inconsistency with the law and the overlap of the authority of the Select Board and Conservation
  30|                     Commission on certain properties.
  31|                  The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2022 to the Finance Committee and
  32|                     Selectmen in early February. Part of the Town Manager's recommendation was to continue to fund the
  33|                     Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the
  34|                     amount of $180,418, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, fund the regular
  35|                     Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000, and creation of Health Insurance Stabilization Fund to
  36|                     prepare for the FY 2023 increase due to 2 years of significant savings from Holiday Health Insurance
  37|                     Premiums. The FY2022 budget also included the hiring of an additional Full Time Patrolman, an
  38|                     additional Full Time Firefighter, restoration of a facilities technician position, additional hours for the
  39|                      Assistant Town Clerk, a new line for a Board/Committee Clerk expense, a 15% increase for Solid Waste/
  40|                      Recycling Program, and a 2.5% increase for the Lunenburg Public Schools and Monty Tech. The total
  41|                     omnibus budget for FY22 that was approved in May was $39,759,798.
  42|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 21=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|               • The following projects were undertaken during Fiscal Year 2021 at town facilities, including the front
   2|                     entrance roof replacement at the Senior Center, the removal of the underground fuel tanks and
   3|                    installation of new above ground fuel tanks at the DPW Building, paving of the DPW parking lot, the
   4|                    replacement of the culvert at the entrance of the DPW, accessibility improvements and parking lot at the
   5|                     North Cemetery, and the construction of a new building on Holman Street adjacent to the North
   6|                     Cemetery by Monty Tech. The Public Safety Building Carport project came in significantly more than
   7|                    the appropriation due to the costs of materials related to supply chain issues so the Town decided to hold
   8|                     off on this project at this time.
   9|               • In May the Town's energy broker for the Municipal Aggregation Program solicited proposals for
  10|                     electricity prices for the Town's Community Choice Power Supply program and secured a rate with First
  11|                     Point Power of $0.11210 for 36 months starting on November 1, 2021.
  12|               • During fiscal year 2021 there were many projects that the Assistant Town Manager put out a bid
  13|                    including the North Cemetery Parking and Accessibility Improvements, the Assessing Services Software,
  14|                     a second request to dispose of a piece of 671 West Street, Cleaning Services for Town Buildings,
  15|                     Landscaping Services for Town Properties, Moving Services for Library Furniture, Library Re-carpeting,
  16|                     Solid Waste and Recycling Services. Other projects that went out to bid in 2021 include the Fuel Tank
  17|                     Installation at the DPW, the Pleasant Street Bridge Replacement, the "downtown" Complete Streets
  18|                     project, the Entryway Roof Replacement at the Senior Center, the Public Safety Building Carport project,
  19|                     and Municipal Trash Bags.
  20|                    The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2022. Also in
  21|                     June the Board approved a charge to change the Green Communities Task Force to the Green
  22|                     Communities Committee and the Board received notification that the owner, of Aro Estates, Kevin
  23|                     O'Brien, has applied for site eligibility for a 40B project on Howard Street to the State.
  24|               The necessary actions in response to the pandemic continued throughout fiscal year 2021. Changes in
  25|               legislation, continuous changes to capacity limits, COVID policies and procedures, and the rollout of
  26|               vaccines in the latter half of FY 2021 became an integral part of how we needed to approach local
  27|               governance, operations, and public health outreach in vaccination efforts.
  28|               • The limited availability of vaccines due to the number of doses Massachusetts was allocated from the
  29|                     federal government and the high demand was a stress on our community during this time. The Council on
  30|                     Aging assisted seniors with scheduling vaccine appointments and worked with the Nashoba Associated
  31|                     Boards of Health on a list of individuals requesting vaccines at vaccination clinics.
  32|                  The towns within the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health requested state approval to create a regional
  33|                     vaccination clinic, which was approved by the State in April. The impetus for this was that towns that
  34|                     were not in a regional health district were being allotted vaccines by population and this was
  35|                     disproportionate to the limited number each of the towns in the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health
  36|                     was receiving.
  37|               • In March of 2021 the American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) was signed into law by the federal government.
  38|                     This funding, also called the Coronavirus Local Fiscal Recovery Fund had a distribution of $3.507M for
  39|                     Lunenburg. At the inception these funds had very restrictive uses related to the pandemic but over the
  40|                     course of many months the US Treasury issued rules that allowed for flexibility on the uses of these
  41|                     funds.
  42|               • The State extended certain COVID-19 laws such as outdoor seating for restaurants serving liquor and
  43|                     fully remote public meetings.
  44|                                                                                               20

===PAGE 22=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|               • Due to the extensive use of Zoom for public meetings, the Town hired a Remote Meeting Coordinator
   2|                     in September 2020 that was paid for using CARES Act funding. It is expected that virtual meetings
   3|                     will continue in some kind of format and this will be a necessary position going forward.
   4|               • Both the Special Town Meeting and Annual Town Meeting in FY21 required special COVID-19
   5|                     protocols to allow for social distancing, required facemasks, no voice voting, special check-in
   6|                     procedures, and cleaning protocols that added to the time and staff needed to prepare for town meeting.
   7|                   The Town Manager continued to provide weekly COVID-19 updates at the Select Board meetings with
   8|                     local, state, and federal updates.
   9|                Five years has passed since I was appointed your Town Manager and I am thankful for the many residents
  10|                I have met, work with on various committees, and represent on a daily basis overseeing the operations of
  11|                the Town. Similar to last year, the pandemic and neighborly response that is demonstrated by the residents
  12|                of Lunenburg, is inspiring. Thank you to all of our community members who continue to help their fellow
  13|               residents throughout this pandemic.
  14|                I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents
  15|                and business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public. The
  16|                accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without
  17|                the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town
  18|                government turn. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson and Assistant Town Manager
  19|                Julie Belliveau for all your hard work and keeping the office running smoothly. I would also like to thank
  20|                the committee and board members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to
  21|                the residents of town. Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department
  22|                heads that led our various departments in 2021: Thomas Gammel, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire
  23|                Chief; Robert Oliva, DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari,
  24|                Treasurer/Collector; CJ Carroll, Principal Assessor; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA
  25|                 Director; Muir Haman, Library Director; Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk; and all their staff for their talent and
  26|                working together to make our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents and
  27|                businesses that make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  28|                Respectfully submitted,
  29|                Heather R. Lemieux
  30|                 Town Manager
  31|                                                                                               21

===PAGE 38=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                                                 Board of Assessors
   2|                   The Board of Assessors is responsible for establishing the value of property, both real and personal, for
   3|                   taxation purposes for the town. Assessed values are subject to a five year revaluation and interim year
   4|                  adjustments for certification by the Department of Revenue. The Assessors grant all statutory exemptions
   5|                  for Senior and Surviving Spouse, Blind and Veterans. They act on all abatement applications to property
   6|                   values. The Assessors are responsible for defending all appeals to the Appellate Tax Board. They also
   7|                   administer all special assessments qualifying farm and forest management programs. Assessments in
   8|                   Massachusetts are based on full and fair cash value as of January 1st
   9|                   The Assessors analyze all sales for the prior 18 months to determine new values for the interim adjustments
  10|                   in the next fiscal year. Market value has seen increases in the sale of homes in town. Properties have been
  11|                   selling over the assessed valuations reflecting an overall increase to market value of the town. The next town
  12|                   wide recertification of all properties will be in FY 2024. Home sales have been strong and the town may
  13|                   experience some adjustments in valuations for FY 2023.
  14|                   Each property in town has a property record card on file with all the components that go into the valuation
  15|                   of the parcel such as land size, building description, sales information, etc. We recommend taxpayers
  16|                  review their property field card annually to ensure all the information is up to date and correct. The field
  17|                   cards are available in our office or online through the Assessor's Data on the Town website.
  18|                   The Department of Revenue certified the results of assessment data to be within their statistical
  19|                   requirements and the tax bills went out on time. The tax rate went from $18.12 to $17.74 for FY 2021.
  20|                   The office continues the process of mandated cyclical inspections of all properties throughout the town.
  21|                   This is achieved by inspections, visiting the properties, and by means of sales verification, building permits,
  22|                   occupancy permits and abatement inspections. The Board of Assessors strives to maintain accuracy in the
  23|                   assessment to sales ratio as well as assessment uniformity.
  24|                   The office staff strives to continue to provide good service that the Lunenburg taxpayers have come to
  25|                   expect.
  26|                   Respectfully submitted,
  27|                   Richard Letarte, Chairman                   Christine C. Higdon, Member Kevin Fish, Member
  28|                                                                   Town of Lunenburg -Board of Assessors

===PAGE 52=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                                           ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT COMMISSION
   2|               In 2021, the APDC again focused its efforts primarily on the preservation of the old Primary School building.
   3|               Two workshops were held in conjunction with the Select Board to try to find compromise solutions between
   4|               preservation and future development on the site. Chairman McGrath investigated legal remedies for the
   5|               Town's refusal, through lack of Town Meeting appropriation, to fund maintenance for the building and sought
   6|               information on non-profit companies and developers that had experience developing properties like 30 School
   7|               St. The APDC also attracted the support of the Historical Commission in the effort. An application was
   8|               submitted to Mass Historic to include the old Primary School as inventory in the Lunenburg Historic District
   9|               in the National Register of Historic Places.
  10|               In other business, Certificates to Alter were approved for roof work on several contributing properties. The
  11|               commission advised the Town Manager on appropriate repairs to the Ritter Building and Town Hall. Damage
  12|               to the WWII bronze plaque in Memorial Park was assessed and repairs planned. A Facebook page was
  13|               launched to keep interested parties informed of recent developments. Road signs marking the Architectural
  14|               Preservation District perimeter were replaced and correctly relocated. Finally, the APD unsuccessfully
  15|               opposed Warrant Article 24 at the spring Annual Town Meeting, which allows the APDC's decisions
  16|               regarding town-owned property to be appealed by vote at Town Meeting.
  17|                                                                   BUILDING DEPARTMENT
  18|               The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960
  19|               Massachusetts Avenue. The Building Department consists of the following personnel: Casey Burlingame
  20|               serves as the Building Commissioner as well as the Zoning Officer. Lisa Normandin serves as the
  21|                Administrative Assistant to the Building Commissioner. Jack Biery serves as the Electrical Inspector, and
  22|                Gary Williams serves as the Plumbing and Gas Inspector. The Building Department strives to ensure all work
  23|                done in the Town of Lunenburg is performed to state mandated regulations and codes, as well as local Zoning
  24|                By-Laws. This is to ensure that the buildings built or worked on and all other work done in town has a
  25|               minimum level of state regulated safety. In addition to Building, Plumbing, Gas, and Electrical Permits, the
  26|                Building Department also oversees annual inspections for places of assembly, safety inspections for liquor
  27|               serving establishments, and forms of intent required to issue business certificates from the Town Clerk.
  28|               In fiscal year 2021 the following permits were issued:
  29|                663 Building Permits
  30|                575 Electrical Permits
  31|                160 Plumbing Permits
  32|                172 Gas Permits
  33|                1570 total permits issued
  34|                The state of Massachusetts is currently utilizing the 2015 International Building Code and 2015 International
  35|                Residential Code, as amended by Massachusetts. The Town continues to utilize ViewPoint online permitting
  36|                software, and all applications for work requiring a permit can be done online. In addition, paper applications
  37|                are available at the Building Department office.

===PAGE 55=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                    CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   2|               The Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to 3 year terms by the Select Board,
   3|               whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL c. 40 § 8C. The Commission
   4|               enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act; MGL c. 131 § 40 and Chapter 239 of the local bylaws for
   5|               the protection of wetlands. The Commission also manages and controls town owned lands retained for
   6|                conservation and passive recreation use.
   7|               The year 2021 was as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and
   8|               meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family
   9|               upkeep projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals despite the limitations of the continuing
  10|                COVID-19 Pandemic. The Conservation Commission's work continues into the year 2022 to implement the
  11|               recently updated land use policy. This effort was completed by spring of 2021. The Conservation Commission
  12|               continues to implement revisions to the local wetland regulations, which have been in effect for over two years
  13|               to date and have severed the interests of protecting valuable community natural resources very fairly and
  14|                effectively.
  15|                The Commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to
  16|               meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The
  17|                Commission continues to work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the continually changing storm
  18|               water mandates from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary
  19|                codification as well as aiding with the community implementations the "MS4" permit regulations.
  20|                The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans using grants as outlined in the
  21|               last two annual reports. The upcoming management efforts will be concentrated on the RAYNO property on
  22|                Northfield Road and the Hawes property on Hollis Road. The stewardship plan for the Hollis Road property is
  23|                oriented toward enhancing bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the
  24|                DCR "Forestry for the Birds" program. The forest stewardship plans provide environmental management
  25|                plans which include timber stand management. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup of New England
  26|                Forestry Consultants who is a noted forester with years of experience working in Lunenburg and the adjacent
  27|               towns/cities serving municipalities and private landowners. The Commission continues to hold public forums
  28|                on the implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. The Commission expects to
  29|                continue to apply for and receive funding from the Commonwealth of MA to author stewardship plans for our
  30|                 Conservation Areas.
  31|                The Commission was awarded and is implementing a Recreational Trails Program grant providing color coded
  32|                trail markers and coordinated "You Are Here" trail map signs to expand the safe use of Lunenburg's existing
  33|                trail network. Regional trail system map signs would show how trails are interconnected among multiple
  34|                properties. The project will also provide informational kiosks and emergency access gates. During 2021, trail
  35|                maps were developed for, and continue to be refined in detail, for eleven conservation properties. These trail
  36|                maps are posted on the new Conservation Commission Trails website, on informational kiosks on the
  37|                properties, and on "You Are Here" trail map signs at trail heads and trail intersections. The Commission
  38|                would like to express our gratitude for all of the hard working volunteers that work so enthusiastically in
  39|                executing the project.
  40|                The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley Improvement Corporation and Hickory
  41|                Hills Landowners Inc. on the implementations of their lake management plans. These plans set goals and
  42|                action items designed to give a better picture of the overall health of the lakes and define various strategies to
  43|                 ensure continued protection of these valuable resources.
  44|                The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!
  45|                Respectfully Submitted,
  46|                Bob Pease, Chair                                  Richard Bursch, Vice-Chair                                Patricia Adams
  47|                Catie Childs                                              Michael Larouche                                    Jack Rabbitt
  48|                Jeffrey Viviano                               Richard Tremblay, Associate Member
  49|                                                      Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator
  50|                                                                                                54

===PAGE 56=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                              COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|               The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through supportive
   3|               services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts General Law, Chapter 40, §
   4|               8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to develop, coordinate, and conduct
   5|               such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   6|               The FY21 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant and
   7|               Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal Site Coordinator; Elsa Watson and Ann
   8|               Penney, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, David Gallagher, and Peter Tomlinson, MART Van Drivers.
   9|               The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Select Board. The
  10|               FY21 Council on Aging Board members were: Chairperson Deb Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson Jane Rabbitt, Barbara
  11|               Brown, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Pete Lincoln, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John Pesenti, Susan O'Sullivan, and
  12|               Judith Tarbell.
  13|               The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Supporters
  14|               Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett
  15|               Regional Transit Authority.
  16|               We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY21 we had 36 volunteers who contributed
  17|               883.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on Wheels program, program
  18|               leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, and friendly visitors/phone calls. We greatly appreciate their
  19|               service.
  20|                Although the pandemic has made it necessary to change the day to day operations at the senior center, we were still able
  21|               to offer a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, transportation, health and wellness, arts and crafts,
  22|               intergenerational, bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, Memory Café, health
  23|               insurance counseling, and social events both at the senior center and offsite to people age 50+.
  24|               There were approximately 773 people who utilized the senior center in FY21. Those 773 people compiled 17,748 units
  25|               The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
  26|               www.mycommunityonline.com, or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall,
  27|                Library, Post Office, local churches, and various businesses throughout the community.
  28|                We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  29|               supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
  30|               Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provided funding for new
  31|               living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep at the Eagle House
  32|                Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for their time and funding for support of
  33|               programming for our clients.
  34|               In closing, I want to recognize the staff and volunteers at the Eagle House Senior Community Center for their
  35|               unwavering commitment to our Lunenburg elders. Our staff and volunteers are to be commended on their dedication,
  36|                flexibility, and strength that they have shown throughout these challenging times, and I thank you all.
  37|               Respectfully submitted
  38|                Susan Doherty, Director
  39|                Lunenburg Council on Aging
  40|                                                                                               55

===PAGE 58=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|               The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Parks, Cemetery, Sewer, Facilities
   3|               Departments, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and
   4|               equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope of
   5|               services with a high degree of efficiency.
   6|                     "The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town's
   7|                    infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and future social
   8|                    and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for the
   9|                     Town's residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means of
  10|                     disposing of solid waste and wastewater. "
  11|               DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY
  12|               Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything worthwhile can be
  13|               accomplished.
  14|               Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but will not require
  15|               repeated attempts.
  16|               Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
  17|               Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by the men and
  18|               women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a seamless flow of
  19|               administrative support.
  20|               DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  21|               Public Works staff is either working or on call 24 hours a day 365 days a year prepared to respond to any
  22|               weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise.
  23|               We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure others are
  24|                aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  25|               dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of service.
  26|               Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have highlighted the fact that
  27|               Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency management as first responders.
  28|               Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required
  29|                services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  30|                This department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of the fact that the
  31|               common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals,
  32|               service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling
  33|               operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal,
  34|                cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting everyone in the community. We will continue to
  35|                engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual budget requests, capital
  36|               requests, and specific projects.
  37|                DEPARTMENTAL GOALS FOR FISCAL 2022
  38|               • Continue to coordinate administrative directives with the activities of all public service departments and
  39|                     organizations within the DPW to improve service levels. Strive always for a higher level of efficiency.
  40|               • Coordinate with those who work daily in Town facilities to best prioritize capital improvements. DPW
  41|                      plays a major role in the enhancement of the work environment of all Town employees.
  42|                • Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available resources in an effort to
  43|                     obtain credits toward lessening premiums. Safety is our first concern.
  44|                • Encourage and facilitate DPW personnel to obtain and maintain required licensure in order to provide an
  45|                     optimal level of service.
  46|                                                                                               57

===PAGE 59=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                • Hire qualified personnel to fill all vacant positions in a timely manner with the goal of reducing reliance
   2|                      on outside Contractors that currently provide some of the daily functions of the Department.
   3|                • Work diligently to maintain a high level of courteous communication and interaction between members
   4|                      of all boards and commissions.
   5|                • Continue to maintain a high level of courteous and professional communication with residents.
   6|                • Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and plans
   7|                      for rehabilitation.
   8|                 PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
   9|                It had become a high priority goal of the Select Board as Road Commissioners to achieve a pavement
  10|                 condition of 72%-75% town-wide. While State supported Chapter 90 funds remain stagnant, the dedicated
  11|                Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially. Additionally, with the
  12|                infusion of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported by residents, the
  13|                 Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing smooth and safe
  14|                 transportation for our residents and all those travelling through our community.
  15|                 The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the total paving
  16|                value of years past as we attack a nine-million-dollar backlog of repairs.
  17|                 Time spent addressing pot holes and other roadway deficiencies diminish substantially as new paving
  18|                increases. A clear benefit of the dollars expended. There still remains work to do, but expectations are
  19|                high.
  20|                 Funding for the reconstruction of the Pleasant Street Bridge was obtained through the State's Small Bridge
  21|                 Program. Working with our engineering consultant VHB and contractor R. Bates & Son, construction
  22|                 began in May 2021 with an anticipated completion date in August 2021. Work also continues on the
  23|                 Complete Streets Design to enhance walkability in the Town Center, with the expectation of the
  24|                 construction work being put out to bid in spring of 2021.
  25|                 Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be viable due to
  26|                 the extraordinary expense.
  27|                 FLEET MAINTENANCE
  28|                 The DPW Maintenance Division is responsible for the repair and preventative maintenance to all town
  29|                 vehicles and equipment and is carried out by a single, skilled and dedicated fleet mechanic with support by
  30|                 department personnel. High performance police units, first responder fire and rescue apparatus, park,
  31|                 cemetery and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by the
  32|                 Maintenance Division. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore
  33|                more costly. Due to the volatile global market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products
  34|                 continues to be a moving target and therefore difficult to estimate annually.
  35|                 Use of a fleet maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool. Each repair is logged by
  36|                 department and vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database. Every vehicle can be
  37|                 referenced for part numbers, required services, as well and repair costs over the life of the equipment.
  38|                 Preventative maintenance continues to be a most valuable component of the program. Work will continue
  39|                 to update and evaluate a vehicle replacement schedule based upon American Public Works Association
  40|                 recommendations in order to more clearly represent the capital needs of the department.
  41|                 FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  42|                 The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  43|                                 DPW Facility                                                Public Safety Complex
  44|                                 Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                                   Town Hall
  45|                                 Town Beach                                                  Town Common
  46|                                 Ritter Bldg                                                 Teen Center
  47|                                 Old Primary School                                          Veterans Park
  48|                                                                                               58

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                               The Eagle House                                             Public Library
   2|                               All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding the school campus.
   3|               A facilities staff of one skilled, dedicated individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year to
   4|               respond to alarm calls and emergencies at all public buildings within the department's care.
   5|               Collectively the department attempts to keep all buildings compliant with current codes to ensure a safe
   6|               secure work environment. Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential
   7|               of disrupting or disabling necessary services provided to the community. The Facilities Director is a problem
   8|               solver and technician with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades. Together
   9|               with a stable of reliable contractors, the department delivers services directly or oversees those functions
  10|               provided by specialized contractors.
  11|               Moving forward the five year Capital Plan for facilities in 2022, as presented by the department will focus on
  12|               the needs of the public building envelopes utilizing the reports prepared by Vertex as a guide. Protecting and
  13|               maintaining the Town building exteriors is a priority even with the uncertain futures of some of the buildings.
  14|               This department remains keenly interested in the proposed T.C. Passios building reuse plan and how it
  15|               impacts the future use of Town Hall and the Ritter Building. A clearly defined plan for the future use of all
  16|               buildings would facilitate planning of capital expenditures.
  17|               ENVIRONMENTAL
  18|               Storm Water
  19|               Compliance with our EPA NDES MS4 Permit remains and will continue to remain an important function of
  20|               the DPW. Along with the Storm Water Task Force, Land Use Director, and our Storm Water Consultant the
  21|               DPW works to meet the obligations of the permit and required reporting each year.
  22|               The DPW provides maintenance and care of the town's storm water management system. This includes but is
  23|               not limited to: catch basing cleaning, street sweeping, pipe jetting, pipe replacement and swale cleaning. The
  24|               department responds to calls for clogged drains and pipes to help control ponding and flooding.
  25|               Wastewater
  26|               Working with the Sewer Commission, the DPW continues to be responsible for the day to day operation and
  27|               maintenance of the wastewater collection system. Maintenance of the collection system inclusive of 10 pump
  28|               stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but continues to be the responsibility of the DPW. Business
  29|               management, inspections, and permitting remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
  30|               Tree Removal
  31|               Storm damage together with natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning that typically
  32|               exceeds the department's allotted budget for tree removal. The department strives to fulfill each resident
  33|               request but must prioritize work based upon the perceived degree of danger to residents and property. With
  34|               the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established
  35|               through prioritization. Currently, the amount of work far exceeds the funding available for it. As this is the
  36|               case, the DP W will continue to act in a reactionary manner as needs arise. All work aside from brush
  37|               removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the necessity
  38|                of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff works to remove and dispose
  39|               of woody debris within the town's right-of-way along with a town-wide program of selective curbside
  40|               mowing and vegetation clearing. An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the burden of
  41|                the Town by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  42|               Landfill
  43|               The capped, closed landfill off Young's Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
  44|                by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as
  45|                well as groundwater contamination.
  46|                The frequency of the testing reduced through a special permit, as a result of consistently favorable results,
  47|                remained constant throughout the year.
  48|                                                                                               59

===PAGE 61=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                  The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
   2|                  Adjacent to the close landfill is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both
   3|                  determined by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are allowed to store these materials through the
   4|                  procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these
   5|                  materials. Other communities must transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at a substantial
   6|                  cost to the community.
   7|                  On weekends during the fall and spring, DP W personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of
   8|                  brush, leaves, and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and our overall
   9|                  recycling program. To date there has been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long-range
  10|                  remediation of this collection area.
  11|                  Solid Waste
  12|                  Solid waste is collected under the Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program by our new vendor EJ Harvey who
  13|                  replaced Casella in 2021 via a public bid process. An Invitation for Bids was solicited and Harvey was
  14|                  the only bidder for the contract, and after review by the Town was awarded the contract. Under this
  15|                  contract recycling is also collected by EJ Harvey. Our PAYT bag supplier continues to coordinate the
  16|                  effort between supplier and vendor. DPW remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor
  17|                  responsible for curbside pick-up.
  18|                  Lake Shirley Dam
  19|                  In the early 1990's with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, the Lake Shirley Association turned
  20|                   over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
  21|                  maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the
  22|                  Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  23|                   establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella
  24|                  of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA/DEP mandates,
  25|                  compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a
  26|                   professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a
  27|                  positive report and we remain in total compliance.
  28|                  Winter Operations
  29|                  Snow and ice present a major challenge. The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DP W to
  30|                  maintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icing
  31|                  events. To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with
  32|                   flexibility to accommodate the unpredictability of each storm. The expectation of immediate service is
  33|                  high and must be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  34|                   Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the Capital Plan. Financial support
  35|                  is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation ensuring minimal interruption of service due to breakdowns
  36|                   and repairs. In the fall salt is delivered to the DPW facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other
  37|                   communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price. From late summer to early fall vehicle
  38|                  maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, spreaders, and plows for winter operations. During the
  39|                   construction season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects
  40|                  in order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter
  41|                  operations. Typically, DPW staff combines with subcontractors to provide comprehensive snow and ice
  42|                  operations.
  43|                   Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost. For
  44|                   example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing
  45|                  rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically and as allowed by State law, the
  46|                   department's snow removal accounts operate in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost
  47|                   of snow removal operations for an average winter.
  48|                   The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private
  49|                   contractors who tirelessly and successfully challenge the worst nature has to offer regardless of severity or
  50|                   duration.
  51|                                                                                                60

===PAGE 62=== (ocr, 32 lines)
   1|                                                             "DW workers are the original Storm Troopers"
   2|                 PARKS
   3|                In conjunction with the five elected Park Commissioners, DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks and
   4|                 grounds exclusive of school property. While resources of the DPW are available as required, much of the
   5|                town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, inclusive of the playing fields, resulting in significant
   6|                 savings and increased efficiency over an in-house operation. Marshall Park accommodates the High School
   7|                 baseball and softball schedules, Little League, and the Lunenburg Phillies. McNally and Fitzgerald Field
   8|                provide for an active youth program as well. Commissioners have initiated a fee schedule at the fields and
   9|                the Town Beach. The Town Beach is staffed with employees of the Parks and Recreation Department during
  10|                the beach season. Wallis Park remains popular for its playground and basketball courts. Over the next year
  11|                 the Parks and Recreation Department plans to continue adding on programs for all ages, summer programs
  12|                 and camps, family and community events as well.
  13|                 CEMETERY
  14|                 One dedicated Cemetery Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South
  15|                 Cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of
  16|                 the Cemetery Commission. The DP W supplies equipment and support statt for on average torty burials a
  17|                 year and general maintenance of the facilities as required by Town Charter. For fiscal year 2022, the DP W
  18|                 will look to add seasonal cemetery laborer positions to cemetery staff to assist in the care of the cemetery
  19|                 grounds.
  20|                 Having come on board with the DPW in FY2021, I was looking forward to forming strong relationships with
  21|                 the Town's administrative body and residents as well as the many Boards, Commissions, and Committees
  22|                 that help it all function. I believe this has been the case and I will work hard to maintain those newfound
  23|                 relationships. It will be my ongoing goal to provide the highest levels of service that Lunenburg's residents
  24|                 expect and deserve among all the divisions that make up the Department of Public Works. I'm thankful and
  25|                 excited to have been given this opportunity and will continue to serve the Town to the best of my abilities.
  26|                 As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure, our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of
  27|                 Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with
  28|                 the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works.
  29|                 Respectfully,
  30|                 Robert E. Oliva
