===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                           FINANCE COMMITTEE
   2|                                                                               FISCAL YEAR 2022
   3|                Over the course of the last fifteen months Lunenburg's town government, like nearly every other
   4|                organization in the world, has adjusted and adapted to a seemingly endless stream of changes brought on by
   5|                the global pandemic. While the health and safety of all Lunenburg citizens and our employees have remained
   6|                paramount, day-to-day town services have (mostly) continued, if under modified conditions. So too has the
   7|                process of planning and budgeting.
   8|                 Annual Town meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on warrant
   9|                articles that directly impact how we manage and resource our town. The town budget articles are core
  10|                elements and the capstone of a months-long process. Throughout the year the Town Manager and
  11|                Department Heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful conversations about how to best serve the
  12|                town. While developing the budget they consider funding, contracts, and mandates, as well as what the
  13|                community wants and needs to thrive. The Town Manager finalized and presented her proposed FY2022
  14|                budget to the Select Board and Finance Committee in early February. Department Heads each presented
  15|                their budgets over several Finance Committee meetings, this year all done safely through the magic of Zoom
  16|                video teleconference calls. Concurrently the Superintendent of Schools and School Committee developed the
  17|                Lunenburg School District budget, culminating in a public hearing and approval vote on March 17". The
  18|                Finance Committee conducted a public hearing and voted its recommendation on the enclosed budget on
  19|                March 25th
  20|                While the FY2022 budget was developed in anticipation of a post-pandemic environment, the effects of the
  21|                pandemic are likely to reverberate with respect to revenues, available funds, and expenditures through the
  22|                coming fiscal year. Fortunately, Lunenburg has fared reasonably-well financially thus far. Our projections
  23|                and visibility are clearer than they were at both the Annual and Special Town Meetings during Calendar
  24|                 Year 2020. The Finance Committee is optimistic of the town's ability to meet the prioritized needs of each
  25|                department throughout the coming year, while still taking a conservative stance on revenue projections,
  26|                funding for reserve accounts, and heavily scrutinizing all spending increases.
  27|                Factors affecting this outlook include:
  28|                COVID-19 related Grant Funding:
  29|                • $753,293 in remaining available Cares Act funds, expiring in December 2021
  30|                • $588,834 in available ESSER II funding, expiring in September 2023
  31|                • Unknown new/additional funding from state and federal legislation such as the American Rescue Plan
  32|                      Act that was approved in March.
  33|                 Revenue:
  34|                • Relatively flat local aid funding, 0.17% increase in the governor's budget
  35|                • Local receipts have held up well, exceeding FY20 collections by 15.75% thus far
  36|                 One time savings:
  37|                • 75% of Monty Tech's ESSER II funds have been applied to assessments, $40,758 for
  38|                      Lunenburg
  39|                • Health Insurance Savings of $369,334, including a full month "premium holiday"
  40|                This year's budget process has evolved into an exercise in cautious appropriation. Substantial funds available
  41|                 this year, likely won't be in the next. How can we best meet the demonstrated needs of our departments
  42|                while ensuring stability in the future?
  43|                                                                                               62

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|               To that end, since the savings in health insurance will likely be a one-time windfall, we need to plan for these
   2|               costs to be reintroduced in the FY2023 budget. For this reason, the Town Manager has proposed that we
   3|               create and fund a Health Insurance Stabilization Account for future health insurance costs. This temporary
   4|               fluctuation in the budget can be offset with these funds without impacting the trajectory of the operating
   5|               budget in FY2023 and will further fiscal sustainability.
   6|               Other notable budget discussions:
   7|               • The largest part of the town budget is the school budget. It is 56.8% of the town's omnibus budget, at
   8|               $22,566,826 (including Monty Tech assessment). Fortunately, due mainly to the savings in health insurance
   9|               and sustaining COVID19-related grant funding, this year's 2.5% budget increase meets the Superintendent's
  10|               recommended needs assessment. While this is good news, it is tempered by the short- term nature of the
  11|               above-mentioned health insurance savings and grant funding. Further, state aid remains relatively flat, a
  12|               consequence in-part of the Student Opportunity Act which shifted priorities to lower-income communities.
  13|               Without restoration of meaningful increases in Chapter 70 funding, the school budget will be constrained by
  14|               the tax levy in future years.
  15|               • The target budgets for the Police and Fire Departments were an exception to budgets that reflected
  16|                    maintaining existing staffing levels. In 2018 the Police and Fire Chiefs developed long term staffing
  17|                     plans. These plans were created to address the staffing needs of these public safety departments that over
  18|                     the years have not kept up with the growth of the town and translated to additional service calls for our
  19|                     public safety departments. Since 2018 we have adhered to the Five Year Staffing Plan for the Fire
  20|                     Department that adds one additional Full Time Career Firefighter each year until FY2023 and the Ten
  21|                     Year Staffing Plan for the Police Department that adds one additional Full Time Patrol Officer each year
  22|                     until FY2028. The FY2022 preliminary budget proposes an additional Patrol Officer and an additional
  23|                     Career Firefighter to continue to follow these staffing plans. The Finance Committee also noted
  24|                     anticipated costs in future years due to unfunded mandates related to the police reform act.
  25|               • The FY22 Capital Plan will be funded with free cash, unexpended capital, and tax revenues. The
  26|                     FY22 Capital Plan recommendation at this time is to fund 15 out of the 19 prioritized capital
  27|                     project requests totaling $1,597,825. The Town Manager's budget recommends transferring
  28|                     $250,000 from free cash into the special purpose stabilization account, which was created to fund
  29|                     large vehicle and equipment purchases and lessen the dependence on tax revenues in future years.
  30|                • Revenues from property taxes includes a conservative estimate for New Growth, $280,590,
  31|                      which is consistent with trends of the past few years.
  32|               • This year's DPW budget includes an additional facilities staff member and part-time, seasonal
  33|                      help for cemetery maintenance and upkeep. This budget also resumes the practice of annual
  34|                      $50,000 increases for pavement management.
  35|                •     Some of our departments fund regular, ongoing expenses with donations and grants. Those
  36|                      funding sources are not guaranteed but the services they fund are vital.
  37|                Looming decisions about town buildings have also captured the attention of the Finance Committee
  38|                during this budget season. Lunenburg's debt ratio has been repeatedly noted by financial advisors
  39|                as a negative factor in our otherwise very good credit rating. There will be a significant reduction
  40|                in debt service as we pay off the Public Safety Building and Primary School loans in 2024.
  41|                 However, it is likely we will need to take on more debt to cover future needs. Currently the town is
  42|                analyzing a T.C. Passios building restoration project, which is considered in articles in this warrant.
  43|                We will likely need to renovate or replace Turkey Hill Elementary School in the late 2020's or
  44|                 early 2030's. There are no easy answers for debt management.
  45|                                                                                               63

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 27 lines)
   1|                While potential new-debt may fit into the town's current financial policy, any predictions are
   2|               tenuous due to yet-unknown costs and unpredictable interest rates.
   3|               The Finance Committee thanks the Town Manager and all Department Heads for their exemplary
   4|                service to the citizens of Lunenburg. They continually demonstrate genuine care for our community
   5|               and are stalwart leaders in shaping the quality of our lives every day.

===PAGE 66=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                                                                     HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|               The year 2021 was a productive year for the Historical Commission. The commission accomplished a lot in
   3|               spite of the COVID-19 pandemic:
   4|               • Facilitated demolition delay applications and answered questions from the public concerning historic
   5|                    properties.
   6|               • Joined with the APDC to advocate for the preservation of the town owned properties: 30 School Street,
   7|                     which is the 1925 Lunenburg High School, and the Brooks House at 1033 Massachusetts Ave.
   8|               • Worked with the Cemetery Commission on the plan for the restoration of the 19th century
   9|                     Goodrich family cemetery.
  10|               • Partnered with the Lunenburg Historical Society to build a memorial near the desecrated, 19th century,
  11|                     Arnold/Litch cemetery.
  12|               • Partnered with the Historical Society to plan for the restoration and resetting of removed 19th century
  13|                    stone directional markers.
  14|               • Advocated for the Community Preservation Act.
  15|               • Wrote and published articles in the Lunenburg Ledger and Fitchburg Sentinel pertaining to local historic
  16|                     homes under the title "Historically Speaking".
  17|               • Work was done on the town survey of historic homes, particularly the "K" houses documented on the
  18|                     1833 Cyrus Kilburn Map.
  19|               • The Town's 19th century standing stone directional markers and other town monuments received their
  20|                     annual inspection.
  21|               • Research was started on slavery in Lunenburg.
  22|               • Successfully proposed amendments to strengthen and specify reporting requirements of the town's
  23|                     demolition delay bylaw.
  24|                Since the turn of the century, Lunenburg has lost historic houses and buildings at the average rate of 1 per
  25|                year. Among the houses and buildings lost were eight (8) that were on the historic 1833 Cyrus Kilburn map.
  26|                With the town owned historic Brooks House and the 1925 Lunenburg High School on the road to demolition,
  27|                at the present rate of demolition, few historic homes and buildings will be left in Lunenburg in 100 years.
  28|                Strong advocacy by the Select Board and town government, as well as strong public support and involvement,
  29|                would be needed to reverse this trend. The Commission has gained one (1) new member, Megan Lordan. We
  30|                welcome her and her talents and we are sure that she will be a valuable contributor to the Commission's work.
  31|                The Commission presently has five (5) members.
  32|                Submitted by
  33|                Richard L McGrath, Chair

===PAGE 67=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                           Lunenburg Public Library
   2|                 "Libraries store the energy that fuels the imagination. They open up windows to the world and inspire us to
   3|                 explore and achieve, and contribute to improving our quality of life."
   4|                                                                                 -Sidney Sheldon
   5|                 Fiscal year 2021 was filled with disruption, but our library staff focused on improving the value of the library
   6|                 for the residents of Lunenburg. The fiscal year opened with all town facilities closed to the public and it
   7|                 closed soon after we launched one of our most successful summer reading programs ever with an event
   8|                 attended by more than 250 people.
   9|                 Despite so much uncertainty and tumult over the course of the year, the library expanded its resources and
  10|                 worked to finish the first strategic plan for the library in more than 10 years. Our plan ensures that we will be
  11|                 eligible for federal and state grant funding, and we plan to apply for this funding for the FY23 budget cycle.
  12|                 As part of that planning process, the library's mission statement was also revised to more accurately and
  13|                 concisely describe our current efforts:
  14|                            Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community, enriching
  15|                            our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.
  16|                 The plan itself is a document that shows the library's current state and will guide our efforts over the next five
  17|                 years. The plan reflects the high aspirations of the trustees and the staff of the library, and it was only possible
  18|                 with the participation of dozens of community members and hours of effort. The plan is available on the
  19|                 library's website: lunenburglibrary.org
  20|                 We have seen a continuation of the trends that started in FY2020, with increased usage of our digital
  21|                 resources. We have delivered additional selection in that realm in anticipation and in response. We purchased
  22|                 more e-books and e-audiobooks to meet demand, and we added resources like Kanopy, a streaming video
  23|                 service which allows you to view popular and enriching videos from home.
  24|                 Library staff launched a second Instagram account for teens and our digital presence continues to grow. As a
  25|                 result, we have seen different and new faces at the library at programs like our Adult Craft Lab and to take
  26|                home one of our craft kits or STEM learning kits. Although the total number of visits was limited by a
  27|                 number of factors in FY2021, the books, DVDs, magazines, programs, and people that are included in the
  28|                 library resources are still available.
  29|                 We partnered again with Hannaford, this time to bring a cooking series to children and families with the on-
  30|                 staff nutritionist in Lunenburg. Many of our programs this year were also held in the digital realm for the first
  31|                 time. Zoom and Facebook Live enabled us to offer virtual author visits, lectures, art classes and lots more
  32|                 even though people were not meeting in person. We utilized the opportunity to partner with other area
  33|                 libraries to bring cultural enrichment that we might not otherwise have been able to provide.
  34|                 One of the biggest projects of the year was the installation of new carpet throughout the building. Thanks to
  35|                 capital plan funding from the town, all of our aging carpet was replaced. To enable this work, a company
  36|                 specializing in moving libraries was employed to moved our collections and furniture. The work was
  37|                 impressive and the results maintain the impressive appearance of our incredible facility. Most first-time
  38|                 visitors to the library are surprised that the building is now more than 15 years old.
  39|                                                                                               66

===PAGE 68=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                 One of the most repeated feedback received during the strategic planning process was that our community
   2|                 members want increased access to the library. To hear that repeated so many times makes the staff feel
   3|                 valued and we cannot wait to continue to improve and increase access to the library over the coming years!
   4|                Adult Services Notes

   7|                • Offered 4 seasonal reading challenges for adult readers on Beanstack (a virtual reading tracker and

  10|                 o 924 badges earned through completed activities and reading milestones reached.
  11|                • 178,880 total reading minutes logged
  12|                Introduced the My Next Great Read survey, which helps provide personalized reading recommendations to
  13|                anyone who answers a few quick questions.

  15|                 1. Continue offering a diverse selection of programming options to meet the varying comfort levels of our
  16|                 patrons through in-person events, take-home kits, and virtual meetings.

  21|                 Children's Service Notes
  22|                • Beanstack, a web and mobile app used to track independent reading time, enabled us to virtually engage
  23|                 our young readers by encouraging them to read, do active challenges and earn rewards for goals set.
  24|                • In September of 2020 we introduced Al Fresco Story Times which utilized our beautiful outdoor
  25|                 patio. This type of program enabled us to gather with the children and families in person in an outdoor
  26|                 setting. These story times continued to be held weekly until late November of 2020 when the weather got
  27|                 too chilly!
  28|                 • Thanks to the town's appropriation of funds for programming, in November of 2020 we began offering
  29|                 monthly virtual STEM activities with Chris Rodrigues of Renaissance Kids. Children were able to pick
  30|                 up individual kits and then follow along on activities involving science, technology engineering or math via
  31|                 ZOOM.
  32|                 The town allocation of programming funds also enabled us to offer our families two virtual programs
  33|                 provided by Roger Williams Zoo! These awesome animal-focused programs were very popular with the

   1|               LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   2|                                        Mission: Establish, develop, modernize and operate the Town of Lunenburg's
   3|                   FY21                 Public, Government, and Educational (PEG) Access Channels to reflect what
   4|                                        matters to Lunenburg
   5|               Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   6|               Steve Walker, Chair
   7|               Faith Beall, Vice Chair
   8|               Jeff Bajko, Secretary
   9|                Steve Archambault, Member
  10|                Pete Lincoln, Member
  11|               The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the
  12|               Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town's only cable-
  13|               service provider.
  14|               The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
  15|               broadcast programming from both town, school functions and meetings, as well as programming from
  16|               publicly available sources.
  17|                Programming
  18|                CH9 - Public
  19|                Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 70% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY21, up 6% over FY20.
  20|               However, the total number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (outside resources),
  21|                decreased from 140 in FY20 to 91 programs in FY21.
  22|                Local programs included high school sports, band concerts, school events and featured projects. The
  23|               remaining 30% of programming was obtained through other media sources.
  24|                                                                            Channel 9 - Public Channel
  25|                                                                                  ACCESS SHARE
  26|                                                                                    30%
  27|                                                                                                LOCAL
  28|                                                                                                 70%

   2|               34%, Public Service Announcements (PSAs) 18% and Entertainment 15%, making up 67% of all Cho
   3|               programming (see chart). The rise in PSAs was mainly due to state COVID related programs.
   4|                Two of the top three program categories, Informational and PSAs provided viewers with presentations and
   5|                overviews that helped to familiarize them with resources and information.

  18|                CH8 - Government
  19|                Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally broadcast meetings. In addition to several live
  20|                weekly municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other examples of government programs downloaded from
  21|                MassAccess (massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a
  22|                political nature.
  23|                During the COVID pandemic PAC moved from Zoom only Live meeting broadcasts to an in-person and
  24|                Zoom Hybrid model at Town Hall. PAC invested in equipment and technology required to make this new
  25|                setup work. By providing a Hybrid environment, community members have a choice to attend an in person
  26|                meeting or on Zoom.
  27|                Many committees and boards continue to use Zoom only, (not live broadcast) and record meetings to the
  28|                Cloud. These meetings are uploaded from the Cloud to one of the Lunenburg Public Access YouTube
  29|                channels (LunenburgAccess) for later viewing. This has resulted in easy access to a greater number of board
  30|                and committee meetings.

===PAGE 72=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                 CH99 - Educational
   2|                 In May of FY20, PAC added an Educational Channel, Comcast Channel 99. The additional channel has
   3|                 allowed PAC to feature local educational content to viewers in addition to the bi-monthly School Committee
   4|                 meetings. FY21 saw additional content growth as COVID restrictions declined, adding such programs as
   5|                 sports, concerts and special programming. However, most of the channel's content remains to be provided by
   6|                 download resources, including programs such as "Open Mind Storytime",                                      , "Fur Fins and Feathers", and
   7|                "Science Now." PAC continues to focus on content growth on this channel.
   8|                                                                                                           As a member of the MassAccess organization, PAC is
   9|                                                                                                          given access to the Telvue Connect
  10|               MASSOACCESS                                                                                (massaccess.telvue.com) database for sharing productions
  11|                                                                                                          of both state and nationwide programming. Many shows
  12|                                                                                                          downloaded and aired on Channel 9 and Ch99 are sourced
  13|                 from this outlet, including "Democracy NOW", "Off the Shelf", and "In the Toybox. "
  14|                 In FY20, PAC began sharing their own locally produced programs with the MassAccess content database.
  15|                 The results were outstanding! Lunenburg's "Talkin' Baseball" episodes were, and continue to be,
  16|                 downloaded by over 30 media centers around the country. In FY21, a new PAC produced program,
  17|                 "Storytime - The Books We Love" was added to the database and has received a warm welcome from the
  18|                 media community. Storytime was downloaded by over 40 public access stations mostly located in
  19|                 Massachusetts. We are delighted to showcase our programs to the community while using the database to
  20|                 add to PAC content.
  21|                 PAC Manager, staff and committee members regularly attend MassAccess conferences and Meet & Greets to
  22|                 develop relationships and resources with other media centers.
  23|                 Studio and Equipment
  24|                 PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to take advantage of
  25|                 the facilities and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various town
  26|                 departments in the use of public access' resources for their communication needs
  27|                 There were no major studio or Town Hall control room upgrades in FY21, with the exception of outfitting
  28|                 Town Hall for the Zoom Hybrid meetings. This upgrade included the purchase of a large monitor for the
  29|                 Bilotta room, and purchase and configuration of broadcast equipment. There was a purchase of two laptops
  30|                 for videographers to conduct live remote meetings from home, paid for by the CARES ACT.
  31|                 FY22 and future strategies include upgrading the studio lighting in the current studio, while looking to
  32|                 acquiring new studio space. PAC continues to work with Town administration to scope out available space.
  33|                 In the meantime, PAC has gone on many site visits to other access stations to gather important information
  34|                 about studio layout and equipment options.
  35|                 Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  36|                 In FY21, there was one full-time Manager and 5 part-time contract videographers and a contracted technical
  37|                 consultant. Two videographers left the organization by the end of FY21 and will be replaced in FY22.
  38|                 The PAC Manager administers the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all production and
  39|                 programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and inventory
  40|                 administration, advertising, social media and equipment and system troubleshooting. Videographers
  41|                 provided video recording of committee meetings and additional local video assignments and projects.
  42|                  A technical contract consultant worked with the PAC manager to provided technical and training assistance
  43|                 as needed. It is expected that he will remain working for PAC through FY22. However, PAC will be
  44|                 searching for, and has the funds budgeted to hire a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the
  45|                  staff in FY22.

   9|                  Summary
  10|                  FY21 was a "catch up" year for PAC. Programming and services were maintained, while strategic plans
  11|                  were on hold and improvements slow. Due to the pandemic in FY20 PAC began moving to normalcy in
  12|                  FY21 increasing both services and local programming. PAC offered more videos of government meetings
  13|                  on the LunenburgAccess YouTube channel and live Facebook feeds than ever before due to Zoom recorded
  14|                  meetings and broadcasts, as we began to broaden use of additional technology.
  15|                  PAC had to keep up with an everchanging environment in FY21 by creating a Zoom Hybrid meeting room
  16|                  at Town Hall, at the request of the Town Manager. Broadcasting required a new set of procedures and
  17|                  training of videographers. Now, the community can attend a meeting in person, or through Zoom providing
  18|                  improved flexibility in meeting participation.
  19|                  Moving forward, PAC hopes to continue to grow through the addition of videographers, new programs and
  20|                  outreach in FY22. The educational channel has begun to add programs including events and sports at
  21|                  schools, with PAC videographers on location. In addition to school events, the studio produced two
  22|                  programs that were added to the MassAccess database in FY21 proven to be wildly popular around the state.
  23|                  A current goal is to reach out to the community and Town board members to use public access services.
  24|                  With additional staff and videographers, PAC will be positioned to make FY22 a memorable year.
  25|                  Won't you join us as a producer or volunteer? PAC's supportive staff offers production
  26|                  assistance, making it easy to produce your own show. Our community is thriving with talented and creative
  27|                  individuals - now is your time to shine! If producing doesn't appeal to you, then consider "behind the
  28|                  camera" options. Become a volunteer. Volunteers are the "life blood" of media centers around the state, and
  29|                  without that involvement PAC will not be able to grow. Consider volunteering to help provide the
  30|                  necessary resources needed for PAC's continued success. WE NEED YOU!

===PAGE 75=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                                                      SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                                JANUARY 1, 2021 - NOVEMBER 1, 2021
   3|                The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five member committee appointed by the Select Board.
   4|               2021 Committee Members
   5|                Julie Belliveau, Chairperson; Sheila Craigen, Clerk; Laura Boussom, Member at Large
   6|                What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off?
   7|                The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to
   8|                have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs
   9|                such as general office work, custodial, library shelving, Meals on Wheels, kitchen assistance duties, etc., to
  10|                earn the tax abatement.
  11|                The 2021 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to your 3° quarter real estate tax bill.
  12|                Eligibility Requirements
  13|               • Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  14|                • Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1s
  15|               • Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name (60 years+)
  16|                • Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):
  17|                          One person household - $50,000
  18|                          Two person household - $75,000
  19|                • Earnings are exempt from state taxes
  20|                • Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester County
  21|                      Regional Retirement or Massachusetts Teachers' Retirement System
  22|                • Final amount earned will be applied to the 3d quarter real estate tax
  23|                • All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check
  24|                • All Property Tax Workers must reapply and be accepted annually for participation in the program
  25|                2021 Participation
  26|                During 2021, there were six residents who participated in the Program in the following departments:
  27|                • Building/Zoning Department
  28|                • Senior Center/Council on Aging
  29|                • Public Library
  30|                • Treasurer/Tax Collector
  31|                • Assessors
  32|                These individuals as a whole accumulated 359.50 hours amounting to $4,853.25 in real estate tax abatements.
  33|                For more information or to obtain an application, please contact Julie Belliveau, Assistant Town Manager/HR
  34|                Director at 978-582-4130 x150 or jbelliveau@,lunenburgonline.com
  35|                                                                                               74

===PAGE 76=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                                                                           SEWER COMMISSION
   2|                 The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the
   3|                 future, and developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town's Charter,
   4|                responsibility for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the
   5|                 supervision of the Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the
   6|                 operational side of the system to the Department of Public Works Director. The Town of Lunenburg does
   7|                 not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 77% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of
   8|                 Leominster, and the other 23% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
   9|                 The five-member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6,
  10|                 2006. As of 7/1/20, the Commission consisted of Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair,
  11|                 Brett Ramsden-Clerk and Members Mike Deveikis and Carl Luck. Due to Covid-19 restrictions, all
  12|                 meetings were held virtually via Zoom, with the exception of the 6/29/21 meeting that was held in person
  13|                 at the Lunenburg Town Hall.
  14|                 At the 2/23/21 meeting, Carl Luck announced he would be resigning from the Commission, effective
  15|                 4/30/21, after serving for 15 years. Upon his departure with 1 year left of his term, he encouraged a sewer
  16|                 customer to run for his remaining term. The Commission thanked Carl for his many years of service and
  17|                 expertise to the Commission. Carl was involved from the beginning in 2006, working to develop the
  18|                 Comprehensive Wastewater Management Plan, the sewer district by-law and Sewer Service Area. He
  19|                 worked on negotiating the Fitchburg IMA and Highfield Village Allocation. The Commission wished
  20|                 Carl the best and hoped he would enjoy his retirement.
  21|                 The Town Caucus was held in person on 3/15/21 and the nominees were Dave MacDonald - year term
  22|                 and Mike Nault -1 year term. The Town Election was held on 5/15/21 and both candidates won their
  23|                 respective terms. At the May 25, 2020 meeting, the Commission re-elected Dave MacDonald-Chair, John
  24|                 Reynolds-Vice Chair and Brett Ramsden-Clerk, along with Members Mike Deveikis and Mike Nault.
  25|                 FY21 saw the Commission bid farewell to long time DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz, who retired
  26|                 10/31/20 after 30 years of service to the town. The Commissioners thanked Jack for all his support and
  27|                  work to ensure the smooth running of the sewer system.
  28|                 In November 2020, the Commission welcomed Rob Oliva as the new DPW Director and looked forward
  29|                 to working with him to insure a smooth transition. The Commission started working with Rob on
  30|                 implementing the Maintenance Plan developed by Wright Pierce in FY20. During the budget setting
  31|                 process for FY22, the Commission voted to move $100K from Retained Earnings into Capital
  32|                 Expenditures to fund necessary repairs and replacement of equipment, 1/1 (Inflow/Infiltration) repairs and
  33|                 other big-ticket items needed for the smooth running of the pump stations. Specific projects were
  34|                 replacement of a pump at the Meadow Woods station and installation of a new control panel at the
  35|                 Electric Ave. station. In addition, Green Mountain was hired during FY21 to clean, inspect and do grout
  36|                 sealing of the joints on the Elizabeth Street main, to address long term I/I issue brought to light by the
  37|                  Weston & Sampson 1/1 report.
  38|                  At the beginning of FY21, the Commission developed an RFP for the Operation & Maintenance of the
  39|                 sewer stations and put it out for bid. CTE (Chad Empey), who was currently providing excellent service
  40|                 on the stations on a time & materials basis won the bid to continue with a new contract, beginning on
  41|                 9/26/20 and renewing at the end of June 2021. CTE also set up a Generator Maintenance program with
  42|                  Weld, for the upkeep on the pump station generators.
  43|                 The Commission also approved a contract with Flow Assessment for the monitoring and upkeep of the
  44|                  flow meters to Fitchburg and Leominster. In addition, the contract included the lease of equipment, which
  45|                  will be updated, maintained and replaced as needed by Flow Assessment. The accuracy of the meters is
  46|                  crucial since Lunenburg is now billed according to the flows going through those meters.
  47|                  There are currently 909 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 330 which have access
  48|                  to town sewer, but are not yet connected. During FY21, 20 new accounts connected to town sewer. The
  49|                  Commission billed out $999,817 in quarterly sewer bills and had $37,435 of prior year delinquencies
  50|                  added to the owners'          property tax bills for non-payment in December 2020.
  51|                                                                                               75

===PAGE 77=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                 A new Irrigation Policy went into effect on August 1, 2019, which required participants to upgrade to a radio
   2|                 -capable meter that is read remotely. In light of the COVID-19 crisis, the Commission voted to move the
   3|                 deadline for installing a new meter to September 1, 2020. The $50 annual fee was due in June 2020 in order
   4|                 to receive credits on the fall billing. The Commission voted to make changes to the policy in August 2020
   5|                 and changed the name to Deduct Meter Policy, along with clarifying that the owner is responsible for the
   6|                 purchase, installation, accuracy and maintenance of the meters. There were 2 public hearings held on 8/25/20
   7|                 and 9/8/20 for the policy changes, which went into effect on 9/8/20, with 28 participants. At the end of
   8|                 FY Y21, there were 41 participants in the Deduct Meter program. We wish to thank the Lunenburg Water
   9|                 Department for being an integral part in getting the Deduct Meter program off to a good start and for their
  10|                 assistance in obtaining the readings to coincide with the obtaining of the water readings, which results in a
  11|                 more accurate billing.
  12|                 The Commission was notified in January 2021 that Fitchburg Wastewater had updated their Sewer Use
  13|                 Ordinance (Chapter 147) which is incorporated into the Lunenburg Sewer Use Regulations as required by
  14|                 the Inter-Municipal Agreement with Fitchburg. The Commissioners reviewed the requested policy changes,
  15|                 updating the Regulations and had Town Counsel review and comment. The updates were sent back to
  16|                  Fitchburg for their review and comment. Once the changes are approved and finalized, 2 public hearings will
  17|                 be set up in FY 22 for approving the Regulation updates and the Commission will also look at changes to
  18|                 other policies that have been discussed throughout the year.
  19|                 In FY21, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 19 Food Service Establishments with
  20|                 grease traps and interceptors on Town Sewer. The Commission voted to waive the Annual Permit fee for the
  21|                 Grease Trap Program, as many restaurants were still struggling to remain open due to COVID-19
  22|                 restrictions. The "Rules & Regulations for Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments"
  23|                 continues to be a focus of the Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth
  24|                 operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on
  25|                 commercial and public disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease
  26|                 into the system which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down
  27|                 the drain.
  28|                 It had been recommended by the Town Accountant that the transfers to Stabilization Funds of any monies
  29|                  collected and not expended throughout a tiscal year tor specitic purposes would be transterred at the Special
  30|                  Town Meeting tollowing the close of the prior fiscal year. The two funds affected by this change are the
  31|                 Sewer Bank-Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund and the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund. A portion of
  32|                 the connection charges assessed at the time of an application for a permit to connect go into these accounts
  33|                 and target resolving I/I issues and future capacity costs paid to the cities of Fitchburg and Leominster for
  34|                 increased capacities. Therefore, at the Special Town Meeting in November 2020, the amount of $68,097.59
  35|                 was transferred to the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund.
  36|                 At the 5/1/21 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward three articles, all of which were related to the
  37|                 FY22 budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY22, $831,406.99 was
  38|                 appropriated from Sewer Enterprise revenues, $40,765 was transferred from the Stabilization Funds, and the
  39|                 sum of $301,113.71 was appropriated from Retained Earnings for a total appropriation of $1,173,486.10 to
  40|                 operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY22. The amount of $780,279.05 was also transferred to the General
  41|                 Fund to cover the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service on the sewer betterments. The
  42|                 second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $30,929.43,
  43|                 approximately 3% of proposed revenues as the Commission had done for FY21. This fund is to be used to
  44|                 maintain and improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. The
  45|                 third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles
  46|                 passed at Town Meeting.
  47|                 During FY21, the Commission met with several groups, looking to connect to Town sewer. Bright Farms, a
  48|                 lettuce greenhouse business, met several times with the Commission throughout FY21, but withdrew the
  49|                 project in early FY22. A potential 40B project for Howard Street and a development at 5 Chestnut St. were
  50|                  also discussed. The Commission always welcomes interest in new sewer connections and is always willing
  51|                 to work with property owners, developers and engineers to discuss expansion of the sewer system.
  52|                                                                                               76

   1|                                                                          VETERANS' SERVICES
   2|                   The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has the most comprehensive system of assistance offered to Veterans,
   3|                   families and surviving spouses in all of the United States. Every city or town in Massachusetts, in accordance
   4|                   with Chapter 115 of the Massachusetts General Laws, must have available to its citizens a Veterans Service
   5|                   Officer (VSO), either full- or part-time, as determined by the community population, in order to service the
   6|                   needs of its Veterans. Lunenburg has a part-time VSO at the Ritter Building at 960 Massachusetts Ave.
   7|                   I understand this is the year for the new Census; however, respondents to the 2021 Town Census show that there
   8|                   are in excess of 618 residents who claimed either military service or veteran status. On a monthly basis, the
   9|                   Veterans and Widows of Veterans in West Boylston receive in excess of $328,330.74 in Federal Benefits
  10|                   (Veterans Administration) which they mostly put back into the community by paying their taxes and through
  11|                   discretionary spending.
  12|                   Veterans, tamilies or Widows who quality can be provided monthly financial assistance (reimbursement) for
  13|                   specific living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care and pharmaceutical co-payments. With the VSO's
  14|                   approval, the Town of West Boylston provides the financial assistance, but then the Town is reimbursed by the
  15|                   State's Department of Veterans Services (DVS). It is the function of the Veterans' Service Office to identify •
  16|                   qualifying members of the community and assist them with the enrollment into the DVS Program. Additionally,
  17|                   çertain Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from the Veterans Administration (VA).
  18|                   Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.
  19|                  During Fiscal Year 2021 (July 2020 - June 2021), Lunenburg served an average of 10 clients (Veterans, Families or
  20|                  Widows) per month. Lunenburg paid out $71,999.46 in benefits, or an average of $620.77 per client per month.
  21|                  Lunenburg was reimbursed $53,999.60 by the DVS which is 75% of what was given as assistance; the maximum
  22|                  allowable share.
  23|                  Some examples of Veterans Service Office assistance services include:
  24|                  • Enrollment in Chapter 115 State Veterans benefits
  25|                 • Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations
  26|                 • Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges
  27|                 • Housing assistance, including Veteran Shelters and Veterans Homes
  28|                 • Payments of "Welcome Home" bonuses for deployed veterans
  29|                 • Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates
  30|                 • Employment Assistance
  31|                  The Veterans' Service Office also provides information, advice and assistance to any citizen interested in learning of
  32|                   their qualifications for federal benefits, such as:
  33|                 • VA Disability payments and property tax exemptions
  34|                  • Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those Killed in Action, or for those who are declared to be
  35|                       100% disabled from service-connected conditions
  36|                  • VA Pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance and death benefits
  37|                 • VA Medical Care for both physical and psychological needs
  38|                 • Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill)
  39|                  • VA Home Loans
  40|                  • Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veteran or civilian cemeteries)
  41|                  The regular business hours for the Lunenburg Veterans Service Office are: Tuesdays and Thursdays from 1-
  42|                  4pm at 960 Massachusetts Ave, Lunenburg, MA. 01462; Phone: 978-582-4176
  43|                  E-Mail: dlauzon@lunenburgonline.com
  44|                   David P. Lauzon
  45|                   Veterans Service Officer
  46|                                                                                               78

   1|                                                                              BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|               The Board of Health Office is located in the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave and
   3|               Lancaster Ave. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.
   4|               The Board of Health with the assistance of its agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its
   5|               mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2021, reviewing Title V Inspection
   6|               Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints
   7|               and general public health related issues as well as resident concerns.
   8|               In addition, our local Board continued to be tasked with responding to the emerging COVID-19 pandemic. The
   9|               Board of Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever changing guidance and compliance with
  10|               those orders. We continued to conducted compliance checks and responded to complaints. As the pandemic
  11|               continued our office assisted residents and business in understanding testing, quarantine and necessary
  12|               compliance. Because of its size, Nashoba Associated Boards of Health had been able to get limited amounts
  13|               of vaccine and has been able to hold several clinics vaccinating eligible residents. Wash hands frequently,
  14|                wear a mask, and keep a social distance of six feet rule.
  15|               Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our
  16|               agents, other Town departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our
  17|                partner towns in our region.
  18|               This year we processed applications for septic permit lot tests, permits for new construction, new wells,
  19|               repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to
  20|               septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to
  21|               protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  22|               The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba
  23|                Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, nursing services are provided to our
  24|               residents. This year, clinics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu.
  25|               The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito Control
  26|               monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and West
  27|                Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the year. Residents
  28|                who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to: cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-
  29|               3055, completing a request on their webpage at: http://www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health
  30|                office. This service is free of charge to residents.
  31|                With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in-home visits to residents recovering from
  32|               illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  33|                Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  34|                Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the citizens of
  35|                Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service.
  36|                Contact the Board of Health office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

   4|                   the second year of the pandemic, this office continued our adaptation to the changes brought on by

   6|                   • Informed, educated and stayed current with the information from the Department of Public Health to

  18|                   We look forward to continuing our work with Lunenburg's Board of Health to meet the public health,

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                              FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|                The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to
   3|                   providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of
   4|                                                                              the Town of Lunenburg.
   5|                In 2021, your Fire Department responded to 1833 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   6|                Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions                             340                   Emergency Medical 962
   7|                Inspections                                            521                   Other                             10
   8|                 The COVID-19 Pandemic has continued to affect our community and department, though we have been
   9|                 fortunate that we have had minimal impacts to our staff. Regardless of the Pandemic's impact, our personnel
  10|                 do not have the option of "working remote"                      . They continue to be ready to respond no matter the conditions.
  11|                 My thanks go out to the men and women of the department who continue to persevere though this strange
  12|                 time in our history.
  13|                           Current Fire Department staffing is now eight Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and Emergency
  14|                 Medical Technicians, plus one full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The Station is manned
  15|                 with 2-3 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians 24 hours a day, 7 days/week. This coverage is a
  16|                 combination of Career personnel in conjunction with 96 hours per week of per diem coverage. This coverage
  17|                is provided by our Call Firefighter/EMT's staffing the Station at their hourly rate. Staffing the station with
  18|                 dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs.
  19|                           Though we have 24-hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire calls still require
  20|                 more personnel than are on duty in the station. This means we still rely heavily on the dedication and
  21|                 commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. Our Call personnel remain
  22|                 and will remain a vital part of our emergency response for years to come.                                    A new Call Firefighter/EMT must
  23|                 attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training. Members
  24|                 qualifying to drive Fire Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training.
  25|                 On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two years to full complete this training.
  26|                            The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level
  27|                 Ambulance Service. Firefighters Austin Flagg and Carl Gamberdella have obtained their Paramedic
  28|                 2000 hours and requires anywhere from 9-18 months of college level classroom education, clinical time and
  29|                 and recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions when possible. Our goal is to begin providing
  30|                 Paramedic level service to the community sometime in 2022.
  31|                 In 2021, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 521 inspections; this is in addition to multiple fire drills and
  32|                 public education initiatives. By law, we are required to inspect smoke and carbon monoxide detector
  33|                installations when a home is sold. We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas
  34|                 installations. Any business holding an on-site liquor license also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety
  35|                 issues.   In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as
  36|                 well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during
  37|                 2021 include:
  38|                 Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety
  39|                 Education in both the schools and with senior citizens. This grant provides funding for both materials and
  40|                 personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Highlights of activity through
  41|                 this grant include:
  42|                 • Though the Pandemic still affects our interactions with the schools and the elderly, we continue to
  43|                      provide services where and when we can. Both fire safety education for primary school age children and
  44|                      the "Sand for Seniors" Program have been reinstituted.
  45|                 • Personnel assisted with several community events, such as the Lion's Club Thanksgiving Basket
  46|                                                                                               86

===PAGE 88=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                 program, the summer Reopening of the Senior Center and delivering Santa to the Annual Town Christmas
   2|                 Tree lighting.
   3|                • Training to certify personnel as Public Life Safety Educators. In 2021, Firefighter Olivia Hayes
   4|                      completed the Fire Academy's Public Life Safety Educator. This gives us five certified Instructors
   5|                      within the Department.
   6|                 Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of walkway
   7|                 sand for senior citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In partnership
   8|                 with this program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their
   9|                 homes for smoke and carbon monoxide detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to
  10|                 install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant. Despite the Pandemic, we modified our
  11|                 procedures to keep this valuable program operating.
  12|                 It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke
  13|                 detectors.
  14|                 Seven Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive
  15|                  Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space,
  16|                  Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and
  17|                 Recovery. In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/
  18|                 Trench Rescue Trailer.
  19|                 Apparatus replacement has reached a plateau for a few years, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all
  20|                 under 10 years old. The oldest pumper, Squad 3 is approaching 14 years old. In 2021, this unit underwent a
  21|                 mid-life overhaul intended to extend its service life. The overhaul included mechanical work, upgrades to its
  22|                 warning devices and paint/body repairs. The is unit doubles as our Heavy Rescue unit and responds to motor
  23|                 vehicle accidents and special rescue situations as well as firefighting duties.
  24|                 A project to update and replace our firefighting protective clothing (known as turnout gear) was begun this
  25|                 year with both Capital budget funding and an increase in our operating budget. This protective clothing has
  26|                 a maximum service life of 10 years and is often less due to wear and tear. It also has to be cleaned regularly
  27|                 to protect the wearers from cancer causing materials that are often byproducts of today's synthetic materials
  28|                 burning.       The cost of the equipment, like many other things has steadily risen over several years as well.
  29|                 This funding will put our personnel in a good set up equipment with a slightly used backup set. This will
  30|                 allow all of our personnel to remain "in service" if their primary gear has to be cleaned.
  31|                            Grant funding is also allowing us to replace our 20+ year old forest fire protective clothing. This
  32|                 equipment is a lighter weight version of the heavier, bulkier structural firefighting gear. State grant funding
  33|                 will replace this equipment with a dual-purpose type of clothing that it suitable for both brush fires and motor
  34|                 vehicle/technical rescue situations. An added benefit is this will reduce the wear and tear on the Structural
  35|                 firefighting clothing.
  36|                            In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  37|                  outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress
  38|                  me every day. Especially in the face of the ongoing Pandemic. I would also like to thank the other
  39|                 department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help. The level of interdepartmental
  40|                 cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business in invisible to many people, but it is critical to
  41|                  all operations. Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing support of
  42|                  public safety. Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to
  43|                  expect from us.
  44|                  Patrick A. Sullivan
  45|                  Fire Chief
  46|                                                                                                87

   9|                   We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police
  10|                   services to our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the
  11|                   benefits that are realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have
  12|                   been developed with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will
  13|                   strive to deliver police services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while
  14|                   respecting all individual rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes,
  15|                   apprehend criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.

  17|                 • We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.

   4|                    Off. Ben Campbell, Off. Paul Duquette, Off. Brian Watson, Off. Joel Ortega, Off. Esteban Bonilla-Perez
   5|                  Department Review:
   6|                  It is an honor to again provide the Lunenburg Police Department's annual report as the Chief of Police. It
   7|                  has been over a year since I was appointed as the permanent Chief of Police, and to say the least it has been
   8|                  challenging tenure so far. 2021 continued to be a challenging year for the Police Department as we
   9|                  continued to deal with the effects of COVID-19. We were also tasked with navigating the challenges that
  10|                  we have been mandated in accordance with the passing of "An Act relative to justice, equity, and
  11|                  accountability in law enforcement in the Commonwealth," commonly referred to the Police Reform Bill.
  12|                  These events continue to have an effect on our department as it relates to recruiting, retention, and morale.
  13|                  Although faced with these challenges, the department remains committed to being pro-active, and strives to
  14|                  make the Community safe for all.
  15|                  There have been some changes to our department personnel in 2021. Reserve Off. Donald Letarte retired
  16|                  after 49 years of service to the department and community. Off. Letarte was well known throughout the
  17|                  town and region. A plumber by trade, but he was definitely considered a "jack of all trades." Off. Letarte
  18|                  was one of the most dedicated individuals that I have been associated with. Off. Letarte would not hesitate
  19|                  to volunteer his time, nor would he hesitate coming in at 3:00 AM to cover a prisoner watch. We wish
  20|                  Donnie the best of luck, good health, and a long and prosperous retirement. Thank you Donnie, job well
  21|                   done.
  22|                  Off. Kayla Leger accepted a position with the Massachusetts State Police in May of 2021. We thank Kayla
  23|                  for her service, and wish her the best of luck with MSP.
  24|                  We welcomed two new officers to the full time ranks from our reserve ranks. Off. Jeffrey Hill, who is
  25|                  currently assigned to the overnight patrol shift, and Off. Nicholas Marino, who is currently assigned to the
  26|                  evening patrol shift.
  27|                  We have also added a full time officer in accordance with our 10 year hiring plan. Off. Matthew Pingitore
  28|                  was appointed as a full time officer in December, and is currently attending academy, with an anticipated
  29|                  graduation in the spring of 2022. Off. Pingitore graduated from Fitchburg State University with a Bachelor
  30|                  of Science Degree in Criminal Justice. While attending Fitchburg State University Off. Pingitore interned
  31|                  with the department during his senior year. We look forward to Off. Pingitore completing the academy, and
  32|                   being assigned to the Patrol Bureau.
  33|                  As we become fully staffed we anticipate filling our vacant positions. The first and foremost would be
  34|                  filling a vacant sergeant position that would provide us much needed supervision for the patrol bureau. We
  35|                  also anticipate filling a vacant detective position, as well as adding an additional School Resource/
  36|                  Community Outreach Officer.
  37|                  The department received a grant from the Executive Office of Public Safety and Security through the
  38|                  Edward J. Bryne Memorial Justice Assistance Grant in the amount of $36, 404 to purchase Body Worn
  39|                  Cameras (BWC). The BWC's were deployed in June of 2021, and the program is now fully operational.
  40|                   We would like to thank Lunenburg IT System Administrator Dan Nadareski for the assistance and
  41|                  guidance during implementation.

   1|                The department is completing a Certification process through the MA Police Accreditation Commission
   2|                (MPAC). Off. J. Morreale has been working diligently on completing the process, which includes reviewing
   3|                all of the department's Policies and Procedures to assure that they are in compliance with MPAC. We hope to
   4|                have this accomplished by the spring of 2022.
   5|                 We are continually researching for innovative methods on our approach to law enforcement. Recently we
   6|                have been researching a Co-response Model approach to mental health type of calls. This would involve
   7|                 partnering officers) with a mental health clinician to assist with individuals in crisis. This would provide an
   8|                 expertise in the field, and provide necessary guidance and treatment for the individual. The clinician would
   9|                also provide follow up to cases that do not necessarily meet the crisis level, but the individual(s) still need
  10|                 treatment and assistance.

===PAGE 93=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                    As result of the above calls, our officers generated a total of 1,992 written reports. There were 1,050
   2|                    reports involving Incident Based Reporting (IBR), and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS),
   3|                    which is a State and Federal Crime reporting data base. The officers also generated an additional 942
   4|                    written reports that did not fall into the IBR/NIBRS category.
   5|                    According to NIBRS, reported crimes are broken down into three categories, Crimes Against Persons,
   6|                    Crimes Against Property and Crimes Against Society. These are further broken down into Group A
   7|                    and Group B categories to closely match the Uniform Crime Reporting (UCR) program. The
   8|                    Lunenburg Police Department regularly reports IBR information to the State, which in turn reports the
   9|                   information to the Federal Bureau of Investigation.
  10|                    In 2021 there has been a 25% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple
  11|                    Assaults accounting for the majority of the incidents with 32 out of 52 total reported incidents. These
  12|                    numbers appear to have a direct correlation to the Domestic Violence calls that officers have responded to
  13|                    in 2021.
  14|                    In 2021 there has been a 28% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Property. Although we have
  15|                    observed a decrease in property crimes, we have observed an increase in Breaking and Entering into
  16|                    residences and motor vehicles in the last quarter of the year.
  17|                  Training:
  18|                  The department continues to participate in annual in-service training. In addition several officers have
  19|                  attended specialized training in various areas of Law Enforcement. Two areas that the department focused
  20|                  on 2021 was empathy training, and wellbeing training for our members. In the spring the department
  21|                  participated in "Effective Leadership through Emotional Intelligence, Empathetics." In the fall of 2021, Off.
  22|                  J. Mathieu and Off. B: McNamara provided training in "Mental Armor: Resilience and Wellbeing Skills for
  23|                  First Responders." It is our belief that these types of training events are important for long and rewarding
  24|                  careers for our members.
  25|                  Our traffic officer, Off. J. Lachance, has competed the Advanced Crash Investigation course, which
  26|                  provides the department with a certified Accident Reconstruction Investigator. Off. Lachance has also been
  27|                  assigned to the CEMLEC Recon Team, which is the Central MA regional accident reconstruction team.
  28|                  The department conducts annual Use of Force training, which includes de-escalation tactics. In 2021
  29|                  officers were involved in nine (9) use of force incidents, as compared to 10 in 2020. In each Use of Force
  30|                  incident de-escalation tactics, including the use of verbal tactics, were first utilized prior to any Use of Force
  31|                  application. Officers continue to strive to de-escalate confrontations prior to any application of Use of
  32|                  Sgt. S. Zrate, Off L. Melnicki, and Public Safety Coordinator A. Moore completed training for RADkids, a
  33|                  selt- detense program for children. They will be hosting various community based trainings coming up in
  34|                  the spring of 2022. They are also certified RAD instructors, which is a self-defense program for women.
  35|                  RAD classes will also be held throughout 2022.
  36|                  Conclusion:
  37|                  I would like to thank the citizens of Lunenburg for their continued support. Without collaboration with our
  38|                  community we would not be successful. We look forward to the New Year and to continue to bring the very
  39|                  best in policing to our community.
  40|                  I would also like to thank the women and men of the Lunenburg Police Department for their dedication to
  41|                  the community and the work that they perform on a daily basis. The personnel of the department play a vital
  42|                  role in making Lunenburg a great place to live, work and visit for all.
  43|                 Thank you,
