   1|                                                                                PLANNING BOARD
   2|                  Lunenburg's Stormwater Bylaw was updated with new standards, requiring board review and approval for
   3|                  any development/redevelopment project that disturbs one acre or more of land. This bylaw safeguards the
   4|                  public health, safety, welfare and the environment by minimizing adverse impacts of contaminated
   5|                  stormwater both off-site and downstream. These updates are more likely to affect large commercial projects
   6|                  and multi-family dwellings; not the average homeowner wanting to put in a shed, garden, pool, etc. The
   7|                  board approved stormwater permits for an earth removal project at 1325 Massachusetts Avenue,
   8|                  redevelopment of the Subaru dealership on Chase Road, and a land division for seven (7) lots on
   9|                  approximately 18 acres off Holman Street.
  10|                  Seventeen (17) AN Plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law,
  11|                  but still requiring board endorsement) were approved. One of these land divisions was for the creation of
  12|                  eight (8) building lots on approximately 23 acres at 360 Flat Hill Road.
  13|                  Lunenburg has four designated scenic roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road, and
  14|                  Leominster Road. Over the past year you may have noticed building lots, such as those mentioned in the
  15|                  paragraph above, being created on some of these roads. As these are scenic roads, any repair, maintenance,
  16|                  re-construction, or paving work in the Town's right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or
  17|                  the tearing down or destruction of stone walls without consent of the Planning Board.
  18|                  The Town received a Site Eligibility Application for a proposed 40B development off Howard Street and
  19|                  the board provided its comments to the Town Manager. The Planning Board in conjunction with many
  20|                  other boards, commissions, Town officials, and private citizens submitted numerous comments outlining the
  21|                  Town's opposition to the development of 200 apartment units at 400 Howard St. The development of
  22|                  affordable housing is critical to Lunenburg, however, the site in question has significant flaws that concern
  23|                  Town officials.
  24|                  The board received an application for the construction of four 2-unit townhouse buildings at 711
  25|                  Massachusetts Avenue. Tri Town Landing on Youngs Road concluded construction of its buildings with
  26|                  the completion of Baker Brook Apartments, a building consisting of 70 senior rental units.
  27|                  In April 2021, the Economic Development Committee started meeting. The committee's charge is to
  28|                  provide advice and guidance on the state of the business community in the town and ways that Town
  29|                  government can support and sustain the local economy. The committee will begin this process by
  30|                  distributing a survey to the Town's businesses soliciting their input.
  31|                  An article was presented and passed at May 2021 Annual Town meeting to update the Town's parking
  32|                   calculations to reflect current contemporary parking needs.
  33|                  As meetings continued to be held remotely the 2nd and 4th Monday of each month, the board once again
  34|                  thanks the town residents, business owners and applicants for their cooperation and patience during this
  35|                   time.
  36|                  The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be
  37|                  reached at 978-582-4147 extension 5. Various forms are available online at https://lunenburgma.gov/ on the
  38|                  Planning Board's homepage. The Zoning Map, Code of the Town of Lunenburg, MA (which contains the
  39|                  Protective [Zoning] Bylaws), Rules and Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, Rules &
  40|                  Regulations for Site Plan Approval & Special Permits, and Permitting Guidebook are available online.

  10|                                                                    ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
  11|               The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2021. There were several
  12|               applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board
  13|               meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00 PM or ZOOM as
  14|               an alternative on an "as needed" basis.
  15|               The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice-
  16|               Chairman, Members: Hans Wentrup, David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro, Associate
  17|               member. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  18|               The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  19|               1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-
  20|               laws.
  21|               2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  22|               3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General
  23|               Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or
  24|               enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency,
  25|               abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  26|               4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws,
  27|               Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the
  28|               Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can by law
  29|                circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  30|               The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
  31|               Avenue, Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00
  32|               PM Tuesday and Thursday, closed Friday.
  33|               If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421
  34|               during regular business hours. Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at
  35|                www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

===PAGE 100=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                           Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                                           Superintendent's Message
   3|                 I am pleased to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools, a district that I have the honor
   4|                 and privilege to lead. The COVID-19 pandemic has continued to present public education challenges, but
   5|                 the administration, faculty and staff continue to focus on what our students need in order to be successful. I
   6|                 want to express my gratitude for a collaborative approach toward projecting and planning for current and
   7|                 future needs with the Town Manager, Heather Lemieux. As the leader of the district, 1 am fortunate to
   8|                 work with a School Committee that is truly dedicated to serving the best interests of our students and
   9|                 appreciative that the Select Board and Finance Committee work collaboratively with the School Committee
  10|                 and School Department to problem-solve in order to better meet the educational needs of the children of
  11|                 Lunenburg.
  12|                  Throughout the pandemic, the district has remained committed to making progress as outlined in on our
  13|                 Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with our Mission and Vision:
  14|                 The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and
  15|                 passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship,
  16|                 and rewarding lives.
  17|                  The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of
  18|                 attending to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered
  19|                 instruction for deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills
  20|                 necessary for continuous learning for all.
  21|                 The Strategy is a living document which is reviewed annually and revised as needed. Annually in August,
  22|                 the District Leadership Team reviews the Strategy, its objectives and action plans. The Strategy objectives
  23|                  for the next three to four years are:
  24|                  Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered and inclusive
  25|                 by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work for
  26|                 transforming practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning and
  27|                  adapting practice to meet the needs of all students.
  28|                  Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous learning in a
  29|                  climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values.
  30|                  Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication, in order to
  31|                 increase the organization's capacity to execute the district's mission and vision.
  32|                  Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are provided equal
  33|                  access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with an appropriate leve!
  34|                  of support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient, confident, and capable young
  35|                  adults adequately prepared for future challenges.
  36|                  In order to cultivate district-wide leadership in key areas identified in the Strategy for Continuous
  37|                  Improvement, the district continues to create teams to lead our work. Over the last three years we
  38|                  established and have maintained the Behavioral Health Advisory Council, Professional Development
  39|                  Committee, and Equity Advisory Council. During the 2020-2021 school-year we strengthened our general
  40|                  education academic referral process and created a behavioral health referral process. These teams review
  41|                  student cases referred by staff or administration and determine which academic or social/emotional/
  42|                  behavioral interventions may be needed. Intervention plans are developed, implemented and reviewed for
  43|                  impact and progress. In the spring of 2021, K-8 curriculum coordinator positions were created to assist
  44|                  teachers in a specific content area and serve as teacher leaders in curriculum development, selection of
  45|                                                                                               99

===PAGE 101=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                resource materials and professional development. The K-8 curriculum coordinators, high school department
   2|                heads, and principals serve as members of the Curriculum Cabinet. All of these teams provide the
   3|                infrastructure to execute the work outlined in the Strategy.
   4|                 Before the 2020-2021 school-year came to a close, we were already preparing for 2021-2022 with optimism
   5|                 as the pandemic seemed to be waning. However, August 2021 saw case counts climbing again. We had
   6|                successfully implemented protocols to keep students and staff safe through 2020-2021, including the return
   7|                to five days per week in-person instruction on campus from April through June 2021. Those protocols were
   8|                maintained as we entered the 2021-2022 school-year. We also implemented the Department of Elementary
   9|                 and Secondary Educations' Test and Stay program. This program provides an alternative to quarantine after
  10|                 a possible exposure at school. Those students identified as close contacts were eligible to report to a test site
  11|                at the T.C. Passios building before school for five days to be rapid tested for COVID. Students could then
  12|                safely report to school as long as results were negative. This helped us keep more students in school for in-
  13|                 person learning.
  14|                 For the 2020-2021 school-year, the district procured online assessment platforms knowing there would be
  15|                 no available MCAS data. The district began administering these standardized assessments in October 2020
  16|                in grades K-5 and has continued to administer these assessments in the 2021-2022 school-year expanding
  17|                 administration to include grades 6-8. These assessments provide the schools valuable information which
  18|                allow us to determine if our students are working on grade-level and develop targeted intervention for
  19|                individual and small groups of students who are not working on grade-level. Teachers continue to learn
  20|                 how to utilize these data to inform classroom instruction.
  21|                 Lunenburg Public Schools was accepted to participate in several of the Department of Elementary and
  22|                 Secondary Education's academy programs. We are in the Social Emotional Learning (SEL) Academy,
  23|                 Literacy Academy, and Selecting High Quality Math Materials Network. We have also sought and been
  24|                 awarded just shy of $303,000 in competitive grants including, summer school grants, after school grants,
  25|                technology grants and school health services grants. These grants allow us to provide critical supports for
  26|                students.
  27|                 Behavioral health support for students was an identified need prior to the pandemic, but now is a more
  28|                 critical need exacerbated by the pandemic. We developed a structure for tiered supports including piloting a
  29|               • Tier 1 social emotional curriculum in all grades (K-12); and hiring additional guidance counselors to support
  30|                 Tiers 1 and 2, and licensed social workers and adjustment counselors to provide Tier 3 supports and
  31|                 families. We continued to provide Interface to the community. Interface is a behavioral health referral
  32|                 service available to all residents of Lunenburg at no cost. In 2021-2022 the district added Care Solace, an
  33|                 online resource with live 24/7 concierge meant to assist families and individuals in finding local mental
  34|                 health support. Care Solace is a tool for school staff and families to connect with community-based mental
  35|                 healthcare resources and providers. The vast database of mental health resources includes vetted local
  36|                 therapists and programs.
  37|                 Professional development for staff continues to be a priority as we continually strive for improvement. We
  38|                 have continued our inter-district collaboration with North Middlesex Regional School District by holding an
  39|                 annual joint conference each November. The Lunenburg Professional Development Committee will
  40|                 continue to plan these days with the hope of collaborating with other districts as well.
  41|                 For the 2020-2021 school-year, the district was able to restore a district-wide director-level curriculum
  42|                 position which had been cut in 2012. This position is essential to provide supervision of and assistance to
  43|                teachers in a review of the curriculum, selection of quality curriculum materials, as well as organizing high
  44|                 quality PD. Due to COVID-19, the 2020-2021 school-year was reduced from 180 days to 170 days,
  45|                 therefore, making adjustments to pacing, prioritization, and sequencing of the curriculum necessary. As
  46|                 MCAS was not administered in Spring 2020 due to school closure, the district procured an online
  47|                 standardized assessment tool in order to have the necessary data to measure student growth and
  48|                 performance, and to determine which
  49|                                                                                               100

===PAGE 102=== (ocr, 32 lines)
   1|                 performance, and to determine which students require intervention to close learning gaps. Robust teacher
   2|                training in instructional and assessment technologies, social-emotional needs of students, cultural
   3|                competence, and sharing of best practice was built into the school calendar and provided through graduate
   4|                 courses on campus after school hours. The Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator
   5|                positions have been and continue to be integral to all of this work.
   6|                I wish to acknowledge and thank our staff for their professionalism and flexibility as we have continued to
   7|                 make adjustments in order to provide the best learning experience possible for our students; our parents and
   8|                families for the sacrifices they have made in order to support their students' learning from home; and the
   9|                School Committee, Town Manager, and local boards and committees for their support of the schools
  10|                 throughout the pandemic. Lunenburg has been successful in keeping our schools open and remaining
  11|                focused on improving the ways in which we support our students academically, socially, and emotionally
  12|                 because of the strong partnership the schools have experienced with parents, extended families, and the
  13|                 greater community.
  14|                                               Perseverance is the hard work you do after you get tired of doing
  15|                                                           the hard work you already did. ~Newt Gingrich
  16|                I always conclude my report with the announcement of the Superintendent's Award recipient. The selection
  17|                 process is always difficult as we have many hard-working, high-performing, community service-oriented
  18|                 students.
  19|                 I am delighted to announce Molly Kimball as the recipient of the 2022 Superintendent's Award.
  20|                 Molly's academic accomplishments include: Academic Achievement in Psychology, Honors Biology,
  21|                 Honors English, Latin, Honors Math, and Art. She has been on the Special Honor Roll each year.
  22|                 She has been on the Varsity Hockey Team; having played hockey her entire LHS career, and the Junior
  23|                 Varsity and Varsity Girls Lacrosse Team.
  24|                 Molly is the National Honor Society Vice President. In that capacity she oversees the NHS peer tutoring
  25|                 program at LMHS. She has been a member of Big Sibs Little Sibs, a program that connects our high school
  26|                 students with elementary school students. Molly is also a volunteer for the Learn to Skate program at
  27|                 Wallace Civic Center where she teaches young children to skate. In addition to all these accomplishments,
  28|                 Molly works at Doe Orchards.
  29|                 When asked for a quote that best describes her she offered this Maya Angelou quote:
  30|                 "People will forget what you said, people will forget what you did, but people will never forget the way
  31|                 you made them feel."

===PAGE 104=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                                                                          School Facilities Department
   2|                After 23 years of dedicated service to the School District and Town, John Londa retired from the positon
   3|                Director of Facilities in October of 2021. As was the previous school year, the focus of school maintenance
   4|                and the Facilities Team is to support operating schools in a COVID-19 environment, preventative
   5|                maintenance on heating, ventilation and energy conservation measures and well as life safety.
   6|                COVID-19
   7|                With the school year welcoming in a global pandemic, the district prepared for opening by using tools and
   8|                lessons learned in 2020/2021. The Facilities department reinstalled partitions in school offices, cafeterias,
   9|                upgraded custodial equipment for disinfection, prepared supplies for the teaching environment such as
  10|                additional disinfectant spray bottles and microfiber wipers as well as using robust cleaning protocols to add
  11|                additional disinfection periods during and after the school day. Additionally the Facilities team provided
  12|                COVID-19 assistance with the distribution of masks and kits and testing operations as needed. Indoor air
  13|                 quality assessment and regular changing of air filters were completed and measures taken to confirm that the
  14|                ventilation systems were performing well against the original design.
  15|                Security Upgrades
  16|                 As part of ALICE safety training held on school campuses each school year, modifications were made on the
  17|                Primary and Turkey Hill schools' door hardware. This was needed to provide means to lock classroom and
  18|                office doors in the event of a lockdown other emergency requiring the locking of doors. Work was provided
  19|                by Oak Security.
  20|                Fire Alarm Upgrades
  21|                 Under a State grant for $75,000 the fire alarm devices and main fire alarm panel at the Primary School were
  22|                replaced. This allowed for the new smoke and CO2 detectors to be replaced in every classroom, offices,
  23|                mechanical and storage rooms and created a more consistent and reliable fire alarm signal. Work was provide
  24|                by Norel Fire Services.
  25|                 Water Testing
  26|                 After completing 3 years of district-wide water testing for lead and copper in the drinking water at all schools
  27|                in June 2020, lead was detected in a number of sources at Turkey Hill and the TC Passios building. The
  28|                source of the lead is believed to be solder from joining copper pipes. The teaching staff, parents and students
  29|                 have been made aware of the need to flush water sources at the start of the school day. A fixture replacement
  30|                 program was implemented to remove and replace fixtures as required. Follow up testing occurred at all
  31|                schools in June 2021. In the continued effort to improve water quality within the schools the Facilities
  32|                 department has started a State of Massachusetts grant application to replace existing drinking water stations
  33|                 under the SWIG program or School Water Improvement Grant. If approved the grant will allow up to $3000
  34|                per replacement cost per water station.
  35|                 Energy Savings
  36|                 As a Green Community Grant participant since 2013, the Town has benefited from various awards allowing
  37|                 upgraded energy efficient, Energy Star rated appliances at Turkey Hill Elementary at a cost of $15,092. A
  38|                 LED lighting conversion for Lunenburg Middle/High School was completed in February 2021 with
  39|                 $2,780.12. A replacement domestic hot water heater for the Primary School was complete by April 2021 with
  40|                 $9,900 in green community grant funds. The Facilities Department has applied for the next round of grant
  41|                 funding and will look to continue the effort to reduce cost and environmental impact.
  42|                 Capital Program
  43|                The FX 22 Capital program received funding to begin air conditioning design for Lunenburg Primary School. The subsequent assessment, preliminary design and cost estimate projected a cost of $232,738.31 to provide
  44|                 necessary electrical wiring and equipment to provide air conditioning to the school. The assessment and
  45|                 preliminary design was awarded to DiGiorgio Associates for $10,000.
  46|                 Respectfully Submitted,
  47|                 John C. Murphy, Director of Facilities & Grounds
  48|                                                                                              103

===PAGE 105=== (ocr, 32 lines)
   1|                                                                              Technology Department
   2|                                                                                         2021-2022
   3|                The Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology (IT) Department played a critical role in the
   4|                expansion of the technology in our classrooms. The Department worked with staff and students to redefine
   5|                what classroom instruction looks and feels like for our students.
   6|                The year started with students in a remote hybrid teaching model with every student having access to a device
   7|                 for daily instruction. Reliance on computers in the classroom and in the households were prevalent when
   8|                students were burdening the network system. To support the new network traffic associated with live stream
   9|                 video conferencing, upgraded internet connections were installed at Lunenburg Primary School and Turkey
  10|                Hill Elementary School. Fiber optic lines were run to each building, and new service came online mid-
  11|                January, increasing bandwidth from100Mbps download, 20Mbps upload to 300Mbps download and upload.
  12|                In addition to upgrading the connectivity to the schools, the department was fortunate to receive State funding
  13|                for several replacement & purchase initiatives:
  14|                 School Re-Opening COVID Relief Grant $95,000.00
  15|                 Purchased Chromebooks for Turkey Hill Elementary School
  16|                 Remote Learning Grant $55,735.00
  17|                 Purchased iPads for students in Grades K-2
  18|                 COVID-19 Prevention Grant                             $67,075.00
  19|                 Additional iPads for Grade K-2 students, teacher laptops and student safety content services
  20|                 These grants enhanced the remote teaching experience and then supported the full return of all students in
  21|                 April.
  22|                 Besides expanding educational needs related COVID, the IT Department maintained the typical response to.
  23|                maintain the district's core technology services. Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we
  24|                improve our infrastructure and explore innovations in education technology. With additional federal funding
  25|                 Elementary and Secondary School Emergency Relief I (ESSER 2) funds, two part time technicians were hired
  26|                to assist with the increased daily requests due to additional devices. Then Information Technology Director
  27|                 announced his resignation from his position with the Town and Schools. The district took advantage of this
  28|                 opportunity to hire a consultant to fulfill the Director's responsibilities, as well as to audit current practices,
  29|                 applications and models to measure any areas for growth, improvement and capacity.
  30|                 Respectfully Submitted,
  31|                 Michael Cassidy, Business Manager

   3|                 From the March 1, 2021 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics - District Summary, there are 251
   4|                 students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were
   5|                 257). This represents 15.9% of our total student population of 1,572 students (it was 15.5% last year). The
   6|                 state average from the 2020-21 Selected Populations Report (https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/
   7|                 selectedpopulations.aspx) is 18.9%. From the March 1, 2021 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment
   8|                 Statistics - District Summary), the total number of students receiving services outside the district was 12
   9|                 (last year was 11). Ofthe 12 students, 5 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 4 are in Day Placements
  10|                 and 3 are in Residential Placements. Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE), which serves
  11|                 students ages 18-22 years old, had 3 students enrolled last year. Additionally, from the March 1, 2021
  12|                 report, 146 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg (last
  13|                 year was 111).

  19|                was 0.4% (last year it was 0.4%). The state rate was .6%. Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant

  21|               have any significant discrepancies due to these factors.

  24|                Placements = 13.4%, and for Separate Schools/Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 6.5%. In

  28|                showed the percent of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full

  30|                Separate Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facility) was 17%, and students
  31|                not attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was

  35|                year cycle in which they report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may
  36|                occur at any time during the subsequent three year period as the children leave the preschool
  37|                program. Lunenburg did not have a cohort completion year in 2019-2020.

  39|                education who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results

  41|                state's rate was 60.1% (from information collected in the Spring of 2019).

===PAGE 108=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent
   2|               to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline (2017-2018). Data is reported for all
   3|                initial evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was rated
   4|                90% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 100%.
   5|                Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C,
   6|                found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third
   7|                birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed". Data is
   8|                suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is
   9|                fewer than 10.
  10|                Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%. This
  11|                indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate
  12|                measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon an age appropriate transition
  13|                assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet
  14|                those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg
  15|                was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in compliance.
  16|                Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of
  17|                students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2018-2019 school year and self-reported post-school
  18|                engagement in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through
  19|                three measurements:
  20|                • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  21|                • Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high
  22|                      school.
  23|                • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training
  24|                      program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high
  25|                      school.
  26|                 Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-
  27|                secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2018-2019 school year, state targets for the
  28|                three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 50.2%; Measurement B: 79.6%; and
  29|                 Measurement C: 87.3%. Lunenburg rates are as follows (from reporting year 2018-19): Measurement A =
  30|                 50%, Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C = 75%.

   1|                We are proud of our staff, students, and families and their resiliency during this time and how they came

   8|                                                              Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update
   9|                Lunenburg Public Schools returned to a full learning model for the 2021-2022 school year. Students are
  10|                invested in learning, and teachers are working diligently to evaluate student progress, identify learning gaps,
  11|                make adjustments to practice, and provide scaffolds accordingly.
  12|                 We have implemented structured common planning time for teachers in grade level content areas so that
  13|                teachers may unpack and plan for units of study, align to standards, and look at data and student work. Our
  14|                district literacy specialist and our math instructional specialist meet with teachers during common planning
  15|                time to support curriculum development and help foster student growth. High school department heads
  16|                 structure department meeting time to facilitate the necessary curriculum work needed to move each
  17|                 department forward and improve learning for students.
  18|                 Students in grades K-8 completed the fall and winter iReady diagnostic assessment in both reading and math,
  19|                 and teachers are participating in data cycles to analyze data, plan for targeted support, and adjust instruction.
  20|                 Personalized learning platforms through iReady provide differentiated instruction, systematic practice, and
  21|                scaffolded feedback in both reading and math.
  22|                 The Lunenburg Public School District is participating in the Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS)
  23|                 Literacy Academy and Social Emotional Learning Academy through the Department of Elementary and
  24|                 Secondary Education (DESE). The Literacy Academy provides coaching sessions and technical assistance in
  25|                 specific grade levels for literacy, including a focus on understanding the science of reading and standards-
  26|                 based lesson planning. Both Academies assist our district with creating multi-tiered systems of support with a
  27|                 focus on tier I, II, and III in academics and social emotional learning and behavioral health.
  28|                 A K-5 math council including grade level teachers, special services teachers, EL teachers, and administrators,
  29|                 has been working with DESE's Evaluating and Selecting High-Quality Materials Network to review and
  30|                select a core math curriculum. We are planning to begin a field test and pilot of several programs beginning
  31|                in February and make a final decision regarding purchase this spring.
  32|                 Math teachers in grades K-5 have been working with a math consultant and have garnered new strategies for
  33|                mathematical discourse routines. Twenty staff members from throughout the district, including guidance
  34|                 counselors, social workers, and teachers were trained and have been implementing the Trails to Wellness
  35|                 curriculum, which is an evidence based social emotional wellness program rooted in cognitive behavioral
  36|                ideology.
  37|                 Our Professional Development Committee continues to check-in with the district to evaluate needs and
  38|                 prepare for upcoming professional learning including future book studies and workshops. Our administrative
  39|                team and our curriculum leaders are working with a consultant on workshops pertaining to diversity, equity,
  40|                 and inclusion and plan to proceed with this work to include the entire district staff. The team is also
  41|                 participating in a book study based on the book Caste, by Isabel Wilkerson, which takes a close look at social
  42|                 constructs in the United States. Our team is studying these constructs in connection with our school system
  43|                 and identifying ways we can improve student inclusion and experiences in Lunenburg.
  44|                                                                                              108

   2|                Elementary School, Lunenburg Middle School and Lunenburg High School. The program for grades 1-5

   4|                6-12 provides academic support for students' current classes in addition to support with executive functioning

  12|                                           Food Service Director                                       General workers
  13|                                              Nadine Lorenzen                                           Karolyn Braman
  14|                                             Cook/Managers                                              Carol DeBettencourt
  15|                                             Elin Forgues                                               Danielle Leblanc
  16|                                            John Terho                                                   Eleni Tsiakalos

  18|                                                                                                        Jessica McCarthy
  19|                                                                                                         Krystal Morini
  20|                                                                                                        Jennifer Cotoni
  21|                Since May of 2020, when the United States Department of Agriculture's (USDA) Food and Nutrition Service
  22|                (FNS) approved a request by the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) to allow school
  23|                food authorities flexibility while continuing to feed children for free. Therefore, we can ensure all students
  24|                have access to the nutrition they need during the school day. Our program like many others in the United
  25|                 States continues to struggle with the supply chain breakdown. Our orders are late or shorted sometimes while
  26|                the numbers for breakfast and lunch are growing.
  27|                Lunenburg School Lunch Program is participating in Harvest of the Month. Massachusetts Harvest of the
  28|                 Month program promotes a different Massachusetts-produced food each month in cafeterias and meal sites
  29|                 across the state. The goal is to encourage healthy food choices by increasing students' exposure to seasonal
  30|                foods while supporting local farmers. Harvest of the Month is a great opportunity to celebrate local foods,
  31|                 experiment with new recipes and lead the way in making farm-fresh foods available to students and staff.
  32|                It also provides wonderful opportunities for school food and nutrition staff, educators, school administrators,
  33|                 family members and students to collaborate and celebrate a commitment to serving locally grown foods within
  34|                the community.

===PAGE 111=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                                                  LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                           The Lunenburg Primary School is a student focused, early childhood, learning community serving
   3|                students enrolled in preschool through grade 2. Student ages range from three to eight years of age. Our
   4|                community is composed of students, families, instructional and support staff. Our focus is on the academic,
   5|                social emotional growth, and development of the whole child.
   6|                The October 1, 2021 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education
   7|                was 380 students. With 40 preschool students, 124 kindergarten students, 112 first grade students and 104
   8|                second grade students. Class sizes range in six kindergarten classes from 20-23 students, from 21to 23
   9|                 students in five grade one classes, and from 20-22 in five grade two classes.
  10|                 Our professional development has centered on literacy instruction and Social Emotional Learning this year.
  11|                 We have provided the reading, writing, and phonics resources of the Lucy Calkins Units of Study to our
  12|                instructional staff at all grade levels. In order to provide staff collaboration time to work with new curricular
  13|                resources we added a Common Planning Time by adjusting our building schedule and reallocating existing
  14|                support staff. These two actions have allowed for staff to participate in professional development focused on
  15|                our literacy and mathematics instruction. We have been able to access consultants as well district curriculum
  16|                coordinators so that teachers have an opportunity as a whole grade level to develop and provide evidence
  17|                based instruction, common learning experiences and share effective teaching strategies to support student
  18|                academic growth.
  19|                 Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. We continue to
  20|                implement the Responsive Classroom model in all of our classrooms. Positive Behavioral Interventions and
  21|                 Supports (PBIS) is an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data,
  22|                systems, and practices affecting student outcomes every day. We have adopted a new Social Emotional
  23|                resource, Trails to Wellness which is a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional
  24|                learning. It is designed to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and regulate their
  25|                 emotions.
  26|                 The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure
  27|                 that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in the
  28|                 fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These
  29|                 common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students.
  30|                 Struggling students are identified based on assessment data for access to our current Multi-Tiered Support
  31|                 System (MTSS) in Reading, Math, Speech, Occupational and Physical Therapy
  32|                 The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns. Serving as an advisory
  33|                 group to the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and developing the
  34|                 school improvement plan.
  35|                Members of the Council for the 2021-2022 school year are:
  36|                 Principal: Chad Adams Assistant Principal: Kevin White
  37|                 Teachers: Sarah Curley, Kara Slattery, Nancy Murray, Patti Kilcommins
  38|                 Parents: Meghan Arnold, Shaunna Baril
  39|                 School Committee Representative: Brian Lehtinen
  40|                 We are honored and humbled to be afforded the opportunity to work with our highly dedicated district wide
  41|                 colleagues and a caring community in order to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our
  42|                youngest students:
  43|                 For more information about the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website:
  44|                 https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/
  45|                 Respectfully submitted,
  46|                 Chad Adams, Principal

===PAGE 113=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                        Turkey Hill Elementary School
   2|               Just like the preceding year, 2021 has continued to bring unexpected challenges and solutions related to the
   3|                health epidemic; our school district has adjusted and responded to those challenges to the best of our ability,
   4|                upon the advice of health and academic experts.
   5|               In late January of 2021 we moved to a live-streaming model. That meant that students were all fully remote
   6|                on Mondays, but could opt to attend in person for two days a week. Students who remained fully remote all
   7|                week "attended" class via a Google meet. There was a teacher at each grade who handled the fully remote
   8|                groups. At the start of April we moved to a full return to school model or a fully remote model, dropping the
   9|                hybrid model. The majority of students returned to school, fully masked. Stringent guidelines remained in
  10|                place: 6 feet of space when possible, masks even during recess time, desks and high touch surfaces frequently
  11|                wiped down, socially distanced lunchrooms, and scheduled mask and water breaks outside. We continue to
  12|                have hand sanitizer throughout the building and students use water filling stations for bottles, not water
  13|                bubblers. The number of students who could take the bus was limited, causing a significant increase in drop-
  14|                offs and pick-ups.
  15|                We canceled the fifth grade trip to Nature's Classroom in Beckett that is usually slated for October; however,
  16|                in May we invited Nature's Classroom staff to our school. They conducted several days of outdoor activities
  17|                with fifth graders.
  18|                We participated in the MCAS tests in April and May, per DESE's directive. All three tests (Math, ELA, and
  19|                science) were shortened to one session instead of two per subject. Remote students participated remotely,
  20|                using the Google Meet as a way to monitor the fidelity of the test taking.
  21|                In June the fifth grade celebrated their "completion ceremonies". Local public access television was kind
  22|                enough to record the events so parents could enjoy the assemblies via video.
  23|                All Chromebooks were collected during the last school week to be cleaned and repaired. Each grade level
  24|                held a field day outside for students that included fun activities, games, and picnic lunches. Fully remote
  25|                students were invited to join.
  26|                In late August we reopened to a full in-person model. There is no longer the option for a remote or hybrid
  27|                schedule. Students and staff are still obliged to wear masks inside the building, but they can be removed while
  28|                outside, including recess. We established six lunchtimes so all students can eat in the cafeteria while
  29|                maintaining six feet of distance. We also established three feet of space between desks to lessen the possibility
  30|                of "close contacts" in the classroom.
  31|                We made the decision to pilot a "no homework" policy this year. This is based on our experience last year,
  32|                during the COVID remote time, when there was no homework. Our intention is that students do their best
  33|                 work in school and use after school as an opportunity to be social with friends, take on a new sport or hobby,
  34|               . and not feel stressed about more worksheets or academic demands. We do encourage all students to spend
  35|                time each evening enjoying independent reading. We recognize that there is inequity for students who go
  36|                 home to a variety of situations some of which are more or less conducive to homework, and have chosen to
  37|                mitigate that obstacle. We will review our test scores at year's end to make a decision regarding next year.
  38|                Turkey Hill Elementary School currently has 363 students. There are six sections of grade 3, with a total of
  39|                134 students. There are five sections of grades 4 and grades 5, with totals of 113 students and 116 students
  40|                respectively. There are 26 teachers, 100% of whom are licensed in their assignment area. Our special areas
  41|                include physical education, art, music, technology/library and social emotional learning. We are fortunate to
  42|                have a full time assistant principal, two full time nurses, a psychologist, BCBA, and a full time adjustment
  43|                counselor/social worker.
  44|                                                                                              112

===PAGE 114=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|               Other support personnel who work across the district include a literacy specialist, the director of teaching
   2|               and learning, two EL teachers, a speech and language pathologist, OT, and PT. New this year as a full time
   3|               interventionist and two supporting tutors. They work under the direction of our literacy specialist and
   4|               provide targeted support for small groups of students.
   5|               We have become much more confident about using the Ready platform with all students. This provides
   6|               three assessments a year in both reading and math: October, January, and May. It sets each child on a
   7|               "learning path" and provides instruction personalized to meet the students at their level of need. We
   8|               standardized our schedule this year so that all students have a daily 40 minute intervention block. During
   9|               this time many students use the iReady platform while others participate in small group work to address
  10|               learning gaps. Ready is an excellent way to document student progress in reading and math and identify
  11|                areas of need and next steps.
  12|                Our literacy specialist, interventionist, and two tutors work together to identify students with significant gaps
  13|               in their grade level skills and provide 8-10 weeks of dedicated instruction. They use LLI (Language
  14|               Literacy Intervention) for reading and a variety of supporting materials for math.
  15|                We are happy that all of the students are back in the building, despite the health challenges. We need to be
  16|               together, physically, as a community of learners, to connect and support each other. The students are
  17|                amazing in their resilience and capacity to handle all of the restrictions with grace. Looking forward to the
  18|                promise of 2022 and hopefully a more normalized environment.
  19|                Respectfully Submitted,
  20|                Heidi Champagne, Principal

   8|                                                                           Lunenburg Middle School
   9|                 Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support
  10|                 students' social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated,
  11|                 creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest
  12|                 communication. The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the
  13|                 philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a
  14|                 means by which all students become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in
  15|                 high school and beyond.
  16|                  Lunenburg Middle School is in its sixth year as a co-located middle -high school serving grades six through
  17|                 eight. Effective, student centered middle schools embrace interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable
  18|                 teachers to work with smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and
  19|                 engage students in their preferred learning style, making them more confident and successful.
  20|                 Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of
  21|                 different interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities.
  22|                 One of our goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue
  23|                 exploring as they move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for
  24|                 students that foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond
  25|                 or where their passion may lead them to.
  26|                                                                     Return to full in person instruction
  27|                 The global pandemic that began in March of 2020 has disrupted and interfered with the method of
  28|                 instruction our school community has become accustomed to. Since that time, we have participated in fully
  29|                 remote learning, hybrid model of learning, and in August of 2021, we returned to a full in person learning.
  30|                 The pandemic took a toll on students, teachers and families, both academic and socially and emotionally.
  31|                 Lunenburg Middle school has prepared and planned for the full in person return, and has placed personnel
  32|                 and programs in place to ensure students are serviced in all components of their well-being.
  33|                 Lunenburg Middle School now has a full time social worker to work with students who may experience an
  34|                 increased level of anxiety in returning to the building full time. The social worker also is able to run small
  35|                 groups for students to develop executive function skills, strategies for managing anxiety and stress and help
  36|                 develop social skills. Our social worker has also done an outstanding job with managing and facilitating
  37|                  crisis management and assessing student's well-being and aligning out of school services for students and
  38|                 families.
  39|                                                                                 Athletics and clubs
  40|                  In our return to a full in person model of learning we also returned to extracurricular activities and athletics
  41|                 at LMS. In the fall, we successfully participated in middle school Field Hockey and Boys and Girls Cross
  42|                 Country and this winter we returned middle school basketball after many years of it being eliminated.
  43|                                                                                              115

  13|                learning loss students suffered during the pandemic. The scores above show the percentage of students who
  14|                scored in the " meeting or Exceeding" category and then the column next to it shows or scores in comparison
  15|                with the state scores of schools who service similar grade levels. As you can see, even during the pandemic
  16|                year, LMS scores are above the state percentage in six out of the seven areas tested. We hope to see continued

  18|                student learning gaps.

  23|                student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning even during this unprecedented time of a global
  24|                pandemic. The key components to our success continue to be our talented faculty committed to making a
  25|                positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and learning, and most of
  26|                all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning during this difficult time. Together we
  27|                continue to make the difference in the lives of students.

===PAGE 119=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                             Lunenburg High School
   2|                 Lunenburg High School and its faculty continue to focus on rigorous curriculum, high standards and
   3|                 opportunities for students, both academic and extracurricular, so that every student feels supported and
   4|                 included in our community. LHS faculty strives to reflect on their teaching practices, to learn and to change
   5|                 in order to best serve all of our students.
   6|                                                                    Return to full in person instruction
   7|                 The global pandemic that began in March of 2020 has disrupted and interfered with the method of
   8|                 instruction our school community has become accustomed to. Since that time, we have participated in fully
   9|                 remote learning, hybrid model of learning, and in August of 2021, we returned to a full in person learning
  10|                 model. The pandemic took a toll on students, teachers and families, both academically and socially and
  11|                 emotionally. Lunenburg High school has prepared and planned for the full in person return, and has placed
  12|                 personnel and programs in place to ensure students are serviced in all components of their well-being.
  13|                 LHS added four positions to its faculty in order to support students returning from a global pandemic. Two
  14|                 assistant principals were put in place to support the interim LMHS principal. The assistant principals have
  15|                 been integral in establishing a student centered culture, establishing routines and structures for students to
  16|                 feel safe and supported, and supporting the faculty so they are able to devote their time and energy on
  17|                  student learning.
  18|                 LHS has also added an additional Guidance counselor to assist and support students back to traditional high
  19|                 school schedule. This position has benefited our school community by reinventing the guidance suite to be
  20|                 a student centered area where kids can seek out support or investigate college and careers opportunities.
  21|                 The guidance counselor position also has been helpful in identifying students who are struggling with stress
  22|                  or anxiety or are in need of other supports.
  23|                  LHS added a math teacher to provide additional sections of mathematic courses and reduce the number of
  24|                 students in sections where some students traditionally struggle. The reduction in teacher/ student ratio will
  25|                 allow teacher to provide a more personalized, individualized instruction. We also were able to add a
  26|                 Statistics course and a Math Skills Building course.
  27|                 The additions to the faculty at Lunenburg High School have allowed students a more varied options for
  28|                 course selection, provided support and structure for both students and staff to focus on instruction and
  29|                 learning. LHS looks forward to reporting out on the difference in student learning these positions have
  30|                  made.

   3|               similar grade levels.
   4|               2021 scores were used like a diagnostic, to determine if students had learning gaps due to the closure of
   5|               school during the pandemic.
   6|               We were also very pleased to learn that 7 students at Lunenburg High School received the state's John and
   7|               Abigail Adams Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance
   8|               puts them at the top 25 percent of their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one
   9|               exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English and Math exams.

  20|               system. Seven teams were recognized as MIAA Team Academic Excellence Awards. Six Teams were gold
  21|               level recipients, with a 3.0 team GPA, and one team earned a silver level, with a team GPA of 2.5 or higher:

  24|               able to offer 8 different sports and 16 levels (Varsity, Junior Varsity, and Middle School)

  26|               recognized were Football, Field Hockey and both Boys and Girls Soccer.
  27|               Blue Knights Football was the only team to be recognized out of 277 teams in the state. LHS Girls' Soccer
  28|               was the only team to be recognized out of 331 teams in the state. LHS Field Hockey was 1 of 2 teams out of
  29|               200 to be recognized with the Sportsmanship Award. Boys' Soccer was 1 of 4 teams out of 332 in the state
  30|               to be recognized for the MIAA Sportsmanship Award.

  33|               Sportsmanship Award. Girls Indoor and Outdoor Track Coach Steve Kyajohnian was inducted into the

  40|                ensure a smooth transition for students as they advance grade by grade. This process also ensures that
  41|               students will have and build upon the educational skills that they will need to be successful in future grades.

===PAGE 123=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                When schools across the country shuttered in March of 2020, no one could imagine the long-term effects the
   2|                pandemic would have on our school systems. The summer of 2020 was spent purchasing and installing air filtration
   3|               systems and plexiglass barriers, sourcing mass quantities of PPE and working with local departments of public health
   4|               to determine how to safely bring students and staff into the building. When guidance from the state's Department of
   5|                Elementary and Secondary Education was released, school leaders were faced with rolling out a learning and
   6|               instruction model that had never been attempted before. We would offer a Hybrid Learning Model, and a rotating
   7|               daily schedule that would allow half of the student body to come into the building, while the other half would learn
   8|               Still, the beginning of the 2020-2021 school year was fraught with concern for student, faculty and staff safety and a
   9|               deep-rooted knowledge of how students learn best - in-person. School leaders worked tirelessly to develop a schedule
  10|               that would bring as many students back into the building as possible, safely, while attempting to balance academic and
  11|                vocational in-person learning time. Instructors were provided with additional technology supports and added
  12|                professional development days, to write and create curriculum that could be delivered remotely. This, in itself, would
  13|                be a challenge. How do you teach a student how to prepare and present a meal, or demonstrate fine-motor skills
  14|               required to give a haircut, in a virtual learning environment? How do you assess a student's understanding of
  15|               residential electrical wiring, without having the ability to observe and manipulate his/her work? How can you teach
  16|                students to safely restrain an animal, with no pet interaction? I am proud to say that the Monty Tech instructors rose to
  17|               this incredible challenge, and for much of the 2020-2021 school year, delivered an effective educational program,
  18|                using the resources that were available to them.
  19|                While face-to-face instruction was provided, that, too, looked and felt different to students and staff. On those days
  20|                when students were scheduled to attend in-person lessons, they found things had changed. Traffic patterns were
  21|                rerouted, and signage on floors, hallways and doors reminded them to wear a mask and maintain distance. Seating on
  22|                busses and in the cafeteria was assigned and closely monitored. A place that had always encouraged connection with
  23|                fellow classmates was now insisting on distance. Though it may have been uncomfortable for both students and staff,
  24|                the school wide adherence to these new rules seemed to be a quiet demonstration of their understanding. And on May
  25|                3, 2021, their compliance was rewarded - Monty Tech was allowed to return to full, in-person learning. With just
  26|                over a month remaining in the 2020-2021 school year, students and staff welcomed the change, anxious for the halls
  27|               and classrooms to return to a more normal capacity. While masks, social distancing and numerous hand sanitizing
  28|                stations were still present, the return of students into the building was a welcome sight for all.
  29|                In 2020-2021, it was disappointing that so many traditional annual events had to be cancelled, including VIP, Tour
  30|                Day, College Fair, and Career Awareness Night; however, where possible, the district continued to operate at a high
  31|               level. Examples include:
  32|                To help our faculty and staff return to work, daycare issues had to be addressed, and, as a result, an important
  33|               relationship with the Boys and Girls Club of Fitchburg and Leominster developed. The club provided daycare and
  34|                homework help, Monday through Friday, to school age children of Monty Tech faculty and staff.
  35|                Thanks to more than $670,000 in competitive grant awards, Monty Tech was able to renovate and expand the
  36|                welding/metal fabrication shop and retrofit one science lab. The school launched an all new evening training program
  37|                that provided no-cost training and job placement to qualified participants. With grant funds, we also established a
  38|                Safe and Supportive Schools Team to review school policies and procedures and target those in need of improvement,
  39|                with an overarching goal of providing all students with safe and appropriate learning environments.
  40|                In an effort to build connections with our school supporters and to "show off" the talents of our students, we held our
  41|                first Holiday Sale, which quickly sold out! More than 400 charcuterie trays, designed and handcrafted by our
  42|                 Cabinetmaking students were sold, and all proceeds went to the Class of 2021 Scholarship Fund.
  43|                The 2020-2021 school year was unexpected, challenging, and at times, disappointing, yet there were so many
  44|                moments to be proud of. I remain humbled and honored to lead such an outstanding organization, and thank you for
  45|                your continued support.
  46|                Respectfully submitted, Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director
  47|                                                                                              122

  14|                Class of 2021 Awards
  15|                Members of the Class of 2021 were awarded approximately $200,000 in scholarships, tools, and equipment. The
  16|                Monty Tech Foundation generously provided $35,500 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts
  17|                of $250 to $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $3,250 to the Practical Nursing graduates. Once again, local and
  18|                 state organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
  19|                graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates are
  20|                grateful for this support.
  21|                 Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher education.
  22|                 Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articulation agreements with
  23|                public and private colleges across the country. Approximately 36% of the graduating class of 2021 reported plans to
  24|                 enroll at a 4-ear college/
  25|                 university upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will save both
  26|                 time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  27|                 Financial Report
  28|                In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2020-2021, a great deal of effort was put forth by the
  29|                 School Committee, administration, and staff. The final fiscal year 2020-2021 Educational Plan totaled $28,814,865
  30|                which represents a 0.19% increase over the 2019-2020 Educational Plan. The District's FY21 budget only exceeds the
  31|                minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by $899,640 or 3.5%.
  32|                 The District was audited in December 2021 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson,
  33|                 Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and expects to receive a good report.
  34|                 Grants and Contracts
  35|                 Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many educational and
  36|                 social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2021, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
  37|                 $2,037,076. Programs funded by these grants include: Coronavirus Prevention and Relief, Improving Teacher
  38|                 Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education,
  39|                 Vocational Capital Equipment initiatives, Student Support and Academic Enrichment and Marine Corp Junior Reserve
  40|                Officer Training.

   1|               Academic Achievement
   2|                During the spring of 2021, administration of MCAS examinations was disrupted due to the pandemic and changes to
   3|                educational delivery across the Commonwealth. Here at Monty Tech, 284 Freshman students qualified to take the
   4|               Biology MCAS exam, having successfully completed the Grade 9 Biology course - 3 of whom were classified as
   5|               Remote Learning Only. 349 students in Grade 10 took only the ELA and Math MCAS exam - 39 of those students
   6|               were classified as Remote Learning Only. Finally, 32 Juniors opted to complete both the English and Math MCAS
   7|                exams, in order to qualify them for scholarship opportunities such as the John and Abigail Adams Award.
   8|                  Monty Tech remains committed to providing students with rigorous STEM learning opportunities, and so when an
   9|               opportunity to apply for a Massachusetts Life Science Center grant presented itself, instructors were eager to apply.
  10|               Working closely with the school's Development Coordinator, science instructors proposed the addition of an all-new
  11|                science course that would introduce students to Microbiology and career pathways that are emerging in this recent
  12|                health crisis. After developing a comprehensive proposal, and soliciting (and acquiring) support from business partner
  13|                Bristol Meyers Squibb, the school's MLSC application was approved for funding, infusing the Monty Tech science
  14|                department with approximately $110,000. These funds will be used to renovate a science lab (replicating a biomedical
  15|               facility), purchase all new course materials, equipment and technology, and provide opportunities for instructors to
  16|                attend the National Science Teachers Association's national conference in 2022.
  17|                During the spring of 2021, students at Montachusett Regional                                   % of Total AP Students with Scores 3+
