===PAGE 127=== (ocr, 55 lines)
   1|               Vocational Projects in the District Communities
   2|                2020-2021 challenged vocational instructors to rewrite curriculum, so that any lesson may be delivered effectively
   3|               remotely. For so many years, the value of a Monty Tech vocational education has been our commitment to hands-on
   4|               learning, which, due to the pandemic and an inability to safely have all students in the building, was limited. However,
   5|               instructors across the school worked with local municipalities, community service agencies, and within the building to
   6|               find meaningful opportunities for students to demonstrate their technical skills and proficiency. While projects may
   7|                have been smaller and even taken a bit longer to complete in 2020-2021, their educational value was immense, and we
   8|                are forever grateful to our vocational instructors for their diligence in reimaging vocational education under these
   9|                challenging and unforeseen circumstances.
  10|               Advanced Manufacturing: Beginning 2020-2021, what was formerly known as Machine Technology would now be
  11|                called Advanced Manufacturing, a name that better aligns with local workforce needs, job titles and qualifications, and
  12|                may aide in job placement for qualified students and program graduates. In FY21, program instructors also adopted the
  13|                newly revised frameworks, and reviewed and developed curriculum to address any changes noted. 124 NC3
  14|                Certifications were earned by students, now skilled in Advanced & Precision Measuring Instrument (MI). Advanced
  15|                fusion CAD/CAM software training was completed, and Makerbot Method Carbon was also added to the training
  16|                program. All Freshman students successfully completed safety training, earning an OSHA 10-hour general industry
  17|                certification. Eight Seniors earned co-op placements, working and learning in area machine shops. (Total student
  18|               enrollment: 46)
  19|                Auto Body Collision Repair Technology: Monty Tech Auto Body students and instructors prioritized earning valuable
  20|               credentials in 2020-2021. Students earned the OSHA 10 hour general industry card, EPA 6H Rule spray certification, I
  21|               -Car Pro Level One in Refinishing and I-Car Pro Level One in Non-Structural Repair. Five Seniors also earned co-op
  22|                placements, and were reported to be valuable contributors in local repairs shops in the area. Due to the pandemic, the
  23|                program restricted shop work to that of Monty Tech staff, but remained surprisingly busy throughout the year. Students
  24|               performed quite well at the SkillsUSA District competition, earning a gold medal and a bronze in Collision Repair;
  25|                which several students for the State competition. (Total student enrollment: 62)
  26|                Automotive Technology: Automotive Technology students benefited from the addition of two new certification
  27|               opportunities (digital multimeters and scan tool applications) that were made available to students, using the newly
  28|                purchased Snap-On NC3 certified training equipment. These industry-recognized credentials and the new equipment
  29|                will enhance the students' level of competency within the automotive industry for years to come. Due to the pandemic,
  30|               hands-on learning opportunities and in-house repairs were limited to faculty, staff, school, and student vehicles only;
  31|                yet, surprisingly, the shop remained very busy. Students performed repairs that included transmission replacements,
  32|                intake manifolds, brake work, and various other technical repairs, demonstrating technical skills that are required for
  33|                any service technician in our area. Three Seniors and two Juniors earned co-op placements, while instructors continued
  34|               to utilize e-learning program options to keep the remaining students on track and engaged during the hybrid
  35|               schedule. (Total student enrollment: 66)
  36|                Business Technology: Monty Tech's Business Technology program continues to benefit from a unique partnership with
  37|                Workers Credit Union. The opportunity to participate in teller training and financial literacy workshops has been met
  38|                with great enthusiasm from students, who completed nine modules and earned a financial literacy
  39|               certificate. Instructors, in collaboration with their post-secondary colleagues, continue to refine and develop new.
  40|               articulation agreements. The agreement with Mount Wachusett Community College will provide qualified students with
  41|                college credits at no cost to the student. Students are eligible to receive 24 credits from Mount Wachusett Community
  42|                College. Unfortunately, due to the pandemic, the Gear House School Store and the Monty Tech Greenhouse were not
  43|                operational. Program instructors hope to reopen these important training facilities in the coming year, allowing all
  44|                freshmen, sophomores, and juniors to be successfully trained in the day-to-day operations of retail sales. Four Seniors
  45|                and three Juniors were out on co-op placements during the 2020-2021 school year. (Total student enrollment: 64)
  46|                 Cabinetmaking: Monty Tech Cabinetmaking students are called upon for a number of projects small and large, across
  47|               the campus and beyond. During the 2020-2021 school year, students completed projects that included: designing and
  48|                constructing Barre Cemetery arches, manufacturing button chairs for the town of Royalston's Phineas S. Newton
  49|               Library, and designing and installing cabinetry for the Fitchburg Fire Department kitchen. Student work was featured
  50|                during the school's first-ever Holiday Sale, where 400 charcuterie trays that were designed and handcrafted by our
  51|                students were sold to community members. The items were so well received that they sold out quickly, and community
  52|                members have requested similar sales in the future, so that they may benefit from the creative and beautiful work done
  53|                by these students. Eight Seniors and seven Juniors earned co-op placements, representing the program so well. A
  54|               sophomore student also won gold in the SkillsUSA District Competition. (Total student enrollment: 62)
  55|                                                                                              126

===PAGE 128=== (ocr, 58 lines)
   1|                CAD/Drafting & Design:_In 2020-2021, Monty Tech CAD/Drafting & Design students adjusted quite well to the
   2|                Hybrid Learning Model, and were able to complete the curriculum easily through the online platform. Students were
   3|                also able to achieve a number of valuable industry-recognized certifications, including AutoCAD, Inventor, Revit, and
   4|                Solidworks. While the school's COVID protocol curtailed the number of community service jobs that these students
   5|                could complete, the senior class was able to work on an important community design project for the Town of
   6|                Lunenburg. One student competed in the SkillsUSA States competition, representing the program well, while two
   7|                Senior students were proud to earn coveted co-op placements. (Total student enrollment: 62)
   8|                Cosmetology:The Monty Tech Cosmetology program is one of the only programs that students may leave and
   9|                directly enter the industry, fully-licensed and ready for a career. The state-approved program is rigorous and provides
  10|                students with countless opportunities to refine their cosmetology skills on clientele who understand and appreciate the
  11|               learning environment. During the 2020-2021, instructors collaborated with college partners to develop and sign
  12|                articulation agreements with Mount Wachusett Community College & Quinsigamond Community College that will
  13|                grant qualified students with college credits, should they choose to pursue advanced training and education. 17 out of
  14|                22 Seniors earned their cosmetology licenses, as the Juniors look forward to scheduling their own exams. Seniors also
  15|                earned certifications in COVID Barbicide, and Pravana. Two Seniors were out on co-op, which was beneficial to their
  16|                training, as COVID prohibited the opening of our own on-site Salon until May 2021. (Total student enrollment: 82)
  17|                Culinary Arts: In a typical year, Monty Tech Culinary Arts students and instructors operate a full-service restaurant
  18|                and bakery, serving 90-120 patrons daily. They might also showcase their talents at regional events and fundraisers,
  19|                working side-by-side with some of the area's finest chefs. But 2020-2021 was a different year. Due to COVID, and
  20|                the hybrid schedule, the school's Mountain Room Restaurant opened to offer take-out meals only to faculty and staff,
  21|                three days per week. This also included bakery items and desserts. In April, the restaurant was approved to open to
  22|                the public, though on a very limited basis. Students ans instructors found creative ways to keep busy, applying their
  23|                talents. In February 2021, Culinary Arts students baked more than thousand cookies and chocolates for the Monty
  24|                 Tech Foundation Valentine fundraiser. These "cookie boxes" were extremely popular, providing community members
  25|                with an opportunity to support the school and students, while also enjoying a sweet treat. All proceeds benefitted
  26|                student scholarships. The program also continued to donate leftover baked goods to Our Father's House in Fitchburg.
  27|                Curriculum was reimagined, from a traditional in-person format to the required virtual platform, which proved to be
  28|                challenging for the culinary trade. Instructors were dedicated to their craft and their students and performed well with
  29|                 the resources that were available to them, seeking meaningful opportunities for students to continue to develop in their
  30|                 chosen trade. Junior Students had an opportunity to earn ServSafe Certifications, Sophomores earned the
  31|                Massachusetts Allergen Training, and Freshmen earned their OSHA 10 hour Culinary certification. (Total student
  32|                 enrollment: 93)
  33|                Dental Assisting: Monty Tech Dental Assisting instructors have emphasized the need for students to earn valuable
  34|                industry-recognized credentials, as a means to demonstrate technical skill and proficiency. As a result, all students
  35|                work and prepare for Dental Assisting National Board (DANB) certification exams. In 2020-2021, 93% of
  36|                Sophomores passed the DANB Infection Control exam, and 100% of the class passed the DANB Radiology exam.
  37|                 This excellent pass rate is a true testament to the high quality instruction in the Monty Tech Dental Assisting program,
  38|                 and something the school continues to be so proud of. A total of 14 students (nine Seniors and five Juniors) earned co-
  39|                op placements, while the remaining Juniors and Seniors remained in-house, due to COVID restrictions; affiliation/
  40|                externship experiences in area dental clinics and offices were postponed due to the pandemic. All Seniors &
  41|                 Sophomores received the American Red Cross CPR/AED certification. One Senior won the gold medal at SkillsUSA
  42|                 districts and went on to compete at the state competition. (Total student enrollment: 61)
  43|                 Early Childhood Education: Students and instructors in the Monty Tech Early Childhood Education program are
  44|                 committed to community service activities, but due to the pandemic, were only able to work on in-house on student-
  45|                 centered projects. Three students earned co-op placements, gaining valuable experience working with young children
  46|                 in area daycare and educational centers. Six Seniors earned the National Child Development Associate credential,
  47|                 while all 13 seniors qualified and applied for the Massachusetts Department of Early Education and Care Teacher
  48|                 certification. One student was awarded a Gold medal in the SkillsUSA district competition. In 2020-2021, the Monty
  49|                 Tech Child Care Center continued to operate on a much smaller scale (due to enrollment protocols), providing
  50|                 meaningful hands-on learning opportunities for all students in the Early Childhood Education program. (Total student
  51|                 enrollment: 55)
  52|                 Electrical: Throughout 2020-2021, students and instructors in the Monty Tech Electrical program wired numerous
  53|                 machines, equipment, computers, and lights throughout the school. Students and instructors successfully transitioned

  18|                The Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student's technical education that combines classroom
  19|               instruction with on-the-job-training. The Co-op Program provides students with an opportunity to further develop
  20|               academic, technical and employability skills in an industry work environment. All students are eligible to participate
  21|                in the Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and school grade, attendance and performance requirements. All Co-
  22|                op students have completed the OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course.
  23|                 The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it is a chance to
  24|                gain on-the-job training that improves both their technical                   and employability skills. Additionally,                making industry
  25|                connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The Co-op Program allows students to earn while
  26|                they learn.
  27|                Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner.                                       Co-op students provide a pool of
  28|                temporary       and potential full-time employees who are already trained, thus reducing employer training costs.                                         Co-
  29|                op work hours may be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers. While overall co-op placements experienced
  30|                a slight decline, due primarily to business closures in response to the recent pandemic, qualified students (Juniors and
  31|                Seniors) continue to work, learn and apply their technical skills in businesses across North Central Massachusetts.
  32|                Student Support Services
  33|                The 2020-2021 school year forced the world to respond to a pandemic with ripple effects to be felt for years to come.
  34|                It goes without saying that we needed to pull together, respond, and unite as partners in order to ensure our special
  35|                populations, and all students regardless of identified disability, remained at the center of decisions. Individuals needed
  36|                to be met where they were at and all services provided needed to allow for individual needs to be met in new and
  37|                innovative ways. The health and safety of our school community and their families dictated our decisions and actions.
  38|                As the district responded to the unforeseen COVID-19 related closure, the hybrid model, and reopening events that
  39|                occurred throughout the 2020-2021 school year, it did not go unnoticed that our Student Support Service staft,
  40|                students, and families displayed great resilience as they worked together to address individual needs and tackle these
  41|                challenging times. While we hope to never experience these events again, we did share a number of experiences that
  42|                have proven to increase communication and collaboration. Some of our greatest lessons learned came from this
  43|                experience and we want to extend our sincere gratitude for the efforts put forth during such unprecedented times.
  44|                During the 2020-2021 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided special
  45|                services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two hundred students on Individual
  46|                Education Plans (IEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individualized Section 504 plans. While the
  47|                Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special education, the department provides support and is
  48|                available to all Monty Tech students.
  49|                                                                                              130

===PAGE 132=== (ocr, 56 lines)
   1|                The department includes a full-time nursing staff that responded to, and continues to respond to, COVID-19 data,
   2|                administers medications, performs state-mandated health screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health
   3|                information to the special education team for a student's IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full-time
   4|                school social worker that participates in departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning
   5|                finances, family issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. During the 2020-2021
   6|                school year, the department in conjunction with our School Social Worker, doubled down on our efforts to address
   7|                food insecurity and ensure community agency supports were extended to families in need. The school is also
   8|                fortunate to have on staff a full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial
   9|                evaluation or who require a three-year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech language pathologist,
  10|                who is available to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Our students also
  11|                have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of these
  12|                individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health
  13|                re-entry and transition support services, as well as crisis intervention.
  14|                 The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the District's Special Education Program, which is
  15|                reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and evaluation are
  16|                done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are used to improve the
  17|                special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.
  18|               During the 2020-2021 Montachusett Regional Technical School District's Department of Student Support Services
  19|               made a concerted effort to increase social, emotional, and community based supports. In addition to our current re-
  20|                entry and school based counseling services, Monty Tech and LUK, Inc. joined forces with the help of the Department
  21|                of Public Health to provide services to at-risk students and their families. Further, the district partnered with LUK to
  22|                create a direct pipeline for Telehealth referrals and access to counseling supports. Couple this with the further
  23|                expansion of our partnership with the SHINE Initiative, it has allowed the district to build upon our mission to provide
  24|                opportunities for all students to be engaged, lead, and access the supports necessary to improve their social and
  25|                emotional wellbeing. The Department of Student Support Services has taken the necessary measures to ensure Social
  26|                 Emotional Learning (SEL) is in the forefront of conversations when supporting students as they acclimate to returning
  27|                to school full time after lengthy periods of time experiencing various educational models throughout the pandemic.
  28|                 As we establish a full time school routine, we recognize the experiences all students have had over the past few years
  29|                has varied. Courses and activities designed to promote mindfulness, understanding, and social emotional wellness are
  30|                part of the many course offerings. Yoga and Meditative Art, Visual Arts, Directed Studies with Social Emotional
  31|                 Learning lessons are all part of the efforts being implemented to ensure we address the needs of all students and
  32|                support their individual growth and development in a post pandemic world. More about tiered interventions can be
  33|                 found on our website. However, it is worth noting that we continue to build and refine our services for all students as
  34|                we recognize the importance of developing the whole student and ensuring they are equipped with the necessary to
  35|                 become productive citizens that can compete and adapt in a rapidly changing economy.
  36|                Technology
  37|                 The 2020-2021 school year proved to be a test in many ways for the Monty Tech educational community, requiring
  38|                 flexibility as students and instructors balanced both in-person and remote learning expectations. The shift in
  39|                instructional delivery, however, was no match for the school's Technology Department. Because of forward thinking
  40|                on the part of the department's Director and the school's leadership team, 2020-2021 was the school's 5th year for the
  41|                1:1 Chromebook initiative, so students and instructors had previous experience with the tools and technology needed
  42|                to learn in a remote or hybrid setting.            To support the additional and now required use of this technology, the
  43|                 Technology Department spent countless hours during the school year upgrading the network and increasing the
  44|                 bandwidth coming into the school to sustain video and audio conferencing.
  45|                 Department personnel upgraded the learning experience for students, by purchasing laptops for students and teachers,
  46|                 building new iMacs for the Graphic Communications program, and rebuilding surplus computers for full remote
  47|                students in CAD Drafting & Design, Graphic Communications, Business Technology, and Engineering, so that they
  48|                 may work from home on their vocational assignments. A ZOOM license was purchased, and the Instructional
  49|                 Technologist worked diligently to purchase and deploy software for all teachers. Camcorders, wireless headsets,
  50|                 interactive displays and a variety of apps were purchased, installed, and put into the hands of our educators to engage
  51|                 learners at all levels.
  52|                 Finally, to ensure "business as usual," we invested in Splashtop, VPN client and configured the firewall, so that
  53|                 faculty and staff laptops could be fixed remotely if a software issue presented itselt, and so that the Business Office
  54|                 would have remote access into the school to maintain a secure working environment, even when the building was
  55|                 closed.

   1|                Marine Corps JROTC
   2|                The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) remains one of the school's most
   3|                worthwhile and engaging programs available to our students. This outstanding leadership program, based on the
   4|                foundations of instilling a value of citizenship, service to the United States, personal responsibility, and a sense of
   5|                accomplishment, provides students with countless opportunities to serve as ambassadors in their communities,
   6|                through the routine demonstration of student leadership development, volunteerism, perseverance, and dedication.
   7|                 Due to outstanding performance throughout the year, Monty Tech's Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officers' Training
   8|                 Corps program was, once again, designated as a Marine Corps Honor School. Selection for the Marine Corps Honor
   9|                 School means that the school's Marine Corps JROTC program was in the top 15% of the 68 other programs in
  10|                 Region 1, which includes schools from the mid-Atlantic and Northeast U.S. The selection is based on several criteria
  11|                including:
  12|                • Inspector General's inspection results
  13|                • Number of cadets in the program
  14|                • Number of community service hours conducted
  15|                • Number of public affairs events attended by cadets
  16|                • Number of academic awards presented to cadets
  17|                • Participation in drill team competitions, marksmanship competitions, physical fitness competitions, and field
  18|                      trips that support the growth and development of the cadet
  19|                During the 2020 - 2021 school year, MCJROTC Cadets participated in more than 5,000 hours of community service
  20|                and volunteerism. Cadets assisted the Salvation Army by collecting more than $25,000 in donations, which were
  21|                 used to countless families in need during the holiday season. These donations will go on to support community
  22|                 members that are in need of assistance, especially during the holiday season.
  23|                The Monty Tech MCJROTC has assisted with color guards and participation in community parades, which provides
  24|                 the cadets and community members the opportunity to be involved with civic and veteran ceremonies.
  25|                Finally, the Monty Tech Cadet Corps provided 11 Color Guards for local civic and veteran ceremonies, a highlight
  26|                 for students and instructors alike.
  27|                Monty Tech School of Continuing Education
  28|                The Monty Tech School of Continuing Education had an exciting FY21, receiving the program's first-ever training
  29|                 grants. School leaders applied for and received Career Technical Initiative Grants (Rounds 1 & 3), totaling $290,000,
  30|                as well as a $30,000 Rapid Reemployment Grant. These grants allowed the School of Continuing Education to
  31|                rollout new training programs, offered to qualified participants (must be unemployed or underemployed) at no charge.
  32|                In addition, each participant who successfully completed the training would be offered job placement with a business
  33|                partner. Comprehensive 200-300 hour programs that culminated in industry-recognized credentials in Welding,
  34|                HVAC and Allied Health were developed and advertised. While the Allied Health (CNA) program did not see
  35|                 enough interested participants to field a cohort, HVAC and Welding were successful in their initial endeavor.
  36|                 Program leaders continue to track student progress and placements, and expect to pursue additional funding for future
  37|                 offerings.
  38|                 As we continue to monitor workforce needs and skills gaps, the School of Continuing Education works closely with
  39|                 the MassHire North Central Career Center to determine specific training programs that are most in-demand. Our
  40|                 overarching goal to support the North Central Massachusetts workforce readiness pipeline with an increase in career
  41|                 and licensure courses is evident in new programs that are developed, existing programs that are refined and in
  42|                traditional journeyman/apprenticeship programs that remain a hallmark of our institution.
  43|                 The pivot to a hybrid learning model seemed to be beneficial to both our career/licensure students as well as our
  44|                 personal enrichment students. During the Fall 2020 and Spring 2021 sessions, we realized the resiliency of our
  45|                 students and their willingness to persevere with their training despite the challenges. Our instructors worked
  46|                 tirelessly to maintain training programs timelines.
  47|                  Our medical students' skill set was and continues to be in high demand due to the pandemic. We were able to
  48|                 support our local medical facilities with newly licensed healthcare workers, to include Pharmacy Technicians,
  49|                 Medical Billing and Coding Specialists, EKG Technicians, Phlebotomists, and Patient Care Technicians. We hope to
  50|                 offer another CNA training program in the future, as the need for trained Assistants remains great.

===PAGE 134=== (ocr, 56 lines)
   1|               Practical Nursing Program
   2|               The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely and ethically in a caring
   3|               manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse health care settings.
   4|                On June 24, 2021 a graduating class of 28 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and entered the nursing
   5|               profession. The class has thus far achieved a pass rate of 75% graduates passing the NCLEX-PN exam (National
   6|                Council Licensure Examination for Practical Nurses). Program graduates are currently employed in the health care
   7|               profession throughout Massachusetts, working in various health care settings, such as long-term care, sub-acute care,
   8|               mental health/substance abuse facilities, physician's offices and correctional medicine.
   9|                The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to strengthen the "LN to BSN (Bachelor of Science in Nursing)
  10|               Bridge" relationship with Fitchburg State University (FSU). Former graduates have successfully transferred into seats
  11|               in the LPN to BSN Bridge program at FSU and will continue their education to a Bachelor's of Science in Nursing
  12|               (BSN). Many class applicants cited their attraction to Monty Tech as the opportunity to complete a ten-month
  13|               accelerated program and then bridge to Fitchburg State University. In the seven -year history of the Bridge Program, all
  14|               of Monty Tech's Practical Nursing graduates reaching graduation at FSU have passed their NCLEX-RN exam. These
  15|                former graduates have gone on to work in hospital settings including Lahey, Beth Israel, and Baystate Medical Center,
  16|                while others have pursued advanced degrees as Critical Care Nurse Practitioners, Nurse Educators, and Family Practice
  17|                Nurse Practitioners.
  18|               In 2020-2021, Monty Tech Practical Nursing students were asked to complete patient scenarios in the Sim Lab on a
  19|                weekly basis in Terms 2 and 3. The Faculty Sim team has implemented National League of Nursing (NN) patient
  20|               scenarios and authored several others consistent with the National Council of State Board of Nursing Detailed Test
  21|               Plan. Instructors have also utilized the Sim lab setting to instruct students in developing nursing skills that they may
  22|               not be experiencing in the clinical setting.
  23|               By successfully developing and implementing a vast library of scenarios reflecting INASCAL Standards (International
  24|                Association for Clinical Simulation and Learning), we hope to prepare our students for any experience they may
  25|                encounter as a newly licensed Practice Nurse. In Maternity Sim Lab Boot Camp, for example, students utilize the
  26|               simulation models to imitate the birthing process, from obstetric office visits through labor and common postpartum
  27|                complications. Well newborn care is also taught. Student feedback has been positive regarding these maternity
  28|                scenarios, stating it better prepares them for an unexpected outcome during a delivery. This expanded content also
  29|               better prepares students for NACE (Nursing Acceleration Challenge Exam) success at FSU.
  30|                Substance abuse and the mental health setting job opportunities have increased with more funding being provided to
  31|               combat the opiate crisis. In addition to having specialty clinical rotations, students participate in simulation scenarios
  32|                involving substance abuse and mental health clients, to help to prepare our graduates to work in a substance abuse
  33|                clinical setting.
  34|                The Monty Tech practical Nursing Program recently completed an accreditation visit from the Massachusetts Board of
  35|                Registration in Nursing, highlighting goals that include stabilization of faculty positions and pursuance of ACEN
  36|                (Accreditation Commission for Education in Nursing) accreditation to more accurately reflect the changing
  37|                environment in nursing education.
  38|                Looking Ahead
  39|                Undoubtedly, the 2020-2021 school year was challenging and difficult for students and staff, yet the school community
  40|                demonstrated a remarkable resilience and commitment to education and service that proves, yet again, that Monty Tech
  41|                is truly a special place to work and learn. While adapting curriculum to the needs of all students both at home and in-
  42|                person, adhering to state and federal safety and health guidelines, and finding opportunities for students to demonstrate
  43|                their creativity and technical skills, certainly kept staff busy, there was always an element of improvement, vision, and
  44|                forward-thinking that kept moving the school forward. Successful grant applications, new coursework, and increased
  45|                school-to-business partnerships that came about during this difficult year are a few of the more notable initiatives that
  46|                we expect to have a positive impact on our school and students in the coming years:
  47|                Grant funding to improve and expand training programs: Monty Tech has applied for and received six Skills Capital
  48|                Grants, totaling $2,029,686. School officials anticipate a FY22 application may be forthcoming, and as a result, have
  49|                met to determine which program is in need of a shop renovation, updated equipment, and/or new technology that
  50|                reflects current industry standards. The school's Dental Assisting program, which effectively prepares students to enter
  51|                the dental field may be the next vocational program to benefit from grant funding, should an application be approved.
  52|                Instructors have advocated for a renovated instructional space, as well as new chairs, tools, and equipment, so that
  53|                students are trained using the exact instruments and technology found in area clinics. This improved facility would
  54|                certainly benefit our high school students, and would also allow Monty Tech's School of Continuing Education to
  55|                develop an all-new evening training program, which would help address the critical shortage for trained Dental
  56|                                                                                               133

===PAGE 135=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|               Assistants in North Central Massachusetts. We look forward to an opportunity to apply for grant funding that would
   2|               allow us to extend this training opportunity to young adults in our area, who may not have had the benefit of a vocational
   3|               high school experience.
   4|               New coursework that aligns to STEM career pathways: With the successful application of the FY21 Massachusetts Life
   5|               Science Center Grant, school officials have been working to renovate an outdated science lab to create a more state-of-
   6|               the-art instructional space that replicates a microbiology laboratory. As the school looks to update classrooms and
   7|               vocational areas, it is critical that we do so in a manner that will allow our instructors to deliver information and train
   8|               students in careers that present high-skill, high-wage job opportunities. The proposed Microbiology course, which was
   9|                developed by Monty Tech science instructors as a means to engage students in the sciences, may be more critical now
  10|                than ever betore. Seeking partnerships with biomedical companies in our area, so that our instructors may work and
  11|               learn from skilled scientists will not only benefit our students and strengthen the Monty Tech academic educational
  12|               program, it may also help feed a struggling STEM workforce pipeline. Whether learning from our industry-immersed
  13|                colleagues, or gaining new instructional resources and techniques from colleagues attending the National Science
  14|                Teachers Association's national conference, Monty Tech science instructors are prepared to deliver new course materials
  15|               that engage students in important and timely science concepts.
  16|               Building partnerships for improved job placement: 2020-2021 brought the school's first-ever approval for a Pre-
  17|                Apprenticeship program — a designation that we expect may improve our graduates' chances of retaining employment
  18|               with a framework for upward mobility. While Welding/Metal Fabrication students already enjoy this new appellation,
  19|               school officials may consider seeking approval for other programs in the coming years. To do so, we must first seek and
  20|                acquire an approved Apprentice Sponsor. This sponsorship and collaboration will serve as a testimony to the strength of
  21|                Monty Tech's vocational programming, and will increase student opportunities for employment and continued education
  22|                and training. Business partners that serve on Program Advisory Boards, as Co-Op employers, or who even graduates
  23|                who now own and operate their own businesses may be interested in such a unique collaboration. In an effort to prepare
  24|               students for careers, it is incumbent on us to ensure that training does not stop when a student leave Monty Tech, and an.
  25|               approved apprenticeship would ensure students will have continued support and training as they enter a competitive and
  26|                demanding workforce.
  27|               The Monty Tech School Committee
  28|                The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District Committee is comprised of twenty-two dedicated
  29|               individuals, whose expertise proves invaluable in advising the district's operations, policies, and procedures. Our
  30|                students continue to benefit from the broad scope of their experiences and varying perspectives, and we are thankful to
  31|               the following members of the 2020-2021 School Committee for their outstanding service.

   9|                                                               ANNUAL TOWN MEETING - May 1, 2021
  10|                  This was the second annual town meeting with COVID-19 protocols in place. Poll Pads purchased
  11|                  through a grant allowed voters to be checked in manually or through a scan of their drivers' license.
  12|                  The electronic check in allowed for quick check in as we didn't have to line up by Precinct,
  13|                  significantly reducing wait time. Use of the ID (though not required) made the process more efficient
  14|                  and safer for all concerned.
  15|                  At 9:03 a.m. the Moderator called the meeting to order. He announced that the warrant was properly
  16|                  served and led the assembly in the Pledge of Allegiance. The COVID-19 protocols were quickly
  17|                  reviewed (microphone directions, use of cards for all votes, no shout outs, etc.). The Moderator then
  18|                   called on Peter Beardmore to announce retirements of previous Finance Committee members
  19|                  Michelle Walton and John Henshaw; and also the current members Terri Burchfield and Heather
  20|                  Sroka who are retiring from the committee this year. The Town Manager presented them with
  21|                  flowers in appreciation of their service on this committee as well as several other committees in the
  22|                  past. Then Chris Menard was recognized to announce committee vacancies; and lastly, the Town
  23|                  Manager announced that the library was conducting a survey. The Moderator also reminded the
  24|                  town meeting that Lunenburg will be celebrating its 300% Birthday in 2028 - in just 6 years! Rob
  25|                  Bowen was appointed Deputy Moderator and the Moderator explained the Consent Calendar. And
  26|                  Articles 1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15, 18, 19, 22, 23 were Voted Unanimously.

   1|                                                                         SPECIAL TOWN MEETING
   2|                                                                                 November 16, 2021
   3|                  The Special Town Meeting was called to order at 7:00 P.M. in the Middle/High School Auditorium.
   4|                  The Moderator declared a quorum was present and that the warrant had been served properly.
   5|                  Select Board Chairman Alonzo recognized recently resigned Select Board member James Marino for
   6|                  his forty years Town involvement as a Police Officer/Chief prior to his election to the Select Board.
   7|                  The Moderator went over the ground rules for Town Meeting including COVID-19 precautions and
   8|                  reminded the group that more volunteers are needed for various committees. The Moderator then
   9|                  went over how the Consent Calendar worked and proceeded to advance for consideration Articles 5,
  10|                  9,10, 11, and 12.
  11|                  ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to raise and appropriate, borrow, or transfer from available
  12|                  funds, all sums of money necessary to amend the amounts voted for the Town's FY 2022 Budget, under
  13|                  Article 6 of the May 1, 2021 Town Meeting VOTED UNANIMOUSLY to transfer from Sewer
  14|                  Enterprise Fund the sum of $16,184.31, raise and appropriate the sum of $15,355.69, transfer from line
  15|                  #6/Group Health Insurance the sum of $57,500.00 and to transfer from line #80/Monty Tech Assessment
  16|                  the sum of $21,159.00; transfer to line #15/Medicare the sum of $39,935.00; transfer to line #23/Town
  17|                  Accountant $4,912.00; transfer to line #29/Technology Director the sum of $65,352.00; for a total
  18|                  increase of $31,540.00 in the amount raised and appropriated under Article 6 of the May 1, 2021 Annual
  19|                  Town Meeting
  20|                  FINANCE COMMITTEE RECOMMENDATION: Recommend Approval

  37|                 ARTICLE 14. To see if the town will vote to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter
  38|                 134, entitled "Demolition Delay," Subsections 4-D and 5, as follows, with additions underlined and deletions
  39|                stricken-through; or take any other action relative thereto:
  40|                 134-4 Demolition plan review.
  41|                 D. If the building or structure is determined to be a significant building under this bylaw, the Building
  42|                      Commissioner shall impose a demolition delay of 540 days from the date of notification, in which time the
  43|                      applicant must notify the Historical Commission through written documents that:.
  44|                 During the delay period the applicant must notify the Historical Commission by means of submitting the
  45|                document entitled "Demolition Delay Bi-monthly Report," once every other month, beginning with the first
  46|                day of the second month following the imposition of the demolition delay, that:
  47|                                                                                              152

===PAGE 154=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|               (1) Bona fide, reasonable attempts have been made to sell, preserve, move, rehabilitate or restore said
   2|               building or structure. Evidence shall include:
   3|                    A. a listing of the building with a licensed real estate brokers;
   4|                    B. an estimate of rehabilitation or restoration costs of the building or structure or;;
   5|                    C. an estimate for moving the building or structure in its present condition;
   6|                          D. (2) the name, address and contact information of A a party or parties that have been located who
   7|                    have agreed to preserve, rehabilitate, restore or relocate the building or structure.
   8|               (2)   (3) The applicant has agreed to alternatives to demolition and/or accept conditions noted in the
   9|               applicability determination.
  10|               (3) Any owner failing to submit the bimonthly report on the prescribed schedule shall have the demolition
  11|               delay period extended one day for each day of delay in filing.
  12|               (4) At any time during the delay period, the Historical Commission may advise the Building Commissioner;
  13|                    in writing, to issue a demolition permit without waiting for the period to expire, if the Commission
  14|                    decides that both:
  15|                           A. There is not a reasonable likelihood that any entity is willing to purchase, preserve, rehabilitate,
  16|               restore, or move said structure; and
  17|                          B. For at least six months the owner has made continuing bona fide and reasonable efforts to locate a
  18|               purchaser to preserve, rehabilitate, restore, or move the subject building or structure, and that such efforts
  19|               have been unsuccessful.
  20|               § 134-5 Emergency demolition.
  21|               Nothing in this bylaw shall restrict the Building Commissioner or the Fire Chief from ordering the demolition
  22|               of a significant building determined to present a clear and present danger to the safety of the public, which
  23|               only demolition can prevent. The commission shall be promptly notified of the decision. VOTED (with
  24|               amendment to 134-4 Subsection D(1) italicized) to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter 134, entitled
  25|               "Demolition Delay," Subsections 4-D and 5, with additions underlined and deletions stricken-through; as
  26|               follows:
  27|               134-4 Demolition plan review.
  28|                D. If the building or structure is determined to be a significant building under this bylaw, the Building
  29|                Commissioner shall impose a demolition delay of 540 days from the date of notification, in which time the
  30|               applicant must notify the Historical Commission through written documents that::
  31|               During the delay period the applicant must notify the Historical Commission by means of submitting the
  32|               document entitled "Demolition Delay Bi-monthly Report," once every other month, beginning with the first
  33|               day of the second month following the imposition of the demolition delay, that:
  34|               (1) Bona fide, reasonable attempts have been made to sell, preserve, move, rehabilitate or restore said
  35|               building or structure. Evidence shall include, at least one, but in the discretion of the Historical Commission
  36|               more than one, of the following:
  37|                     A. a listing of the building with a licensed real estate brokers;
  38|                      B. an estimate of rehabilitation or restoration costs of the building or structure or,;
  39|                     C. an estimate for moving the building or structure in its present condition.;
  40|                      D. (2) the name, address and contact information of A a party or parties that have been located who have
  41|                           agreed to preserve, rehabilitate, restore or relocate the building or structure.
  42|               (2) (3) The applicant has agreed to alternatives to demolition and/or accept conditions noted in the
  43|                applicability determination.
  44|                                                                                              153

===PAGE 155=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                   (3) Any owner failing to submit the bimonthly report on the prescribed schedule shall have the demolition
   2|                   delay period extended one day for each day of delay in filing.
   3|                 '(4)         At any time during the delay period, the Historical Commission may advise the Building
   4|                   Commissioner, in writing, to issue a demolition permit without waiting for the period to expire, if the
   5|                   Commission decides that both:
   6|                              A. There is not a reasonable likelihood that any entity is willing to purchase, preserve, rehabilitate,
   7|                                   restore, or move said structure; and
   8|                              B. For at least six months the owner has made continuing bona fide and reasonable efforts to locate
   9|                   a purchaser to preserve, rehabilitate, restore, or move the subject building or structure, and that such efforts
  10|                   have been unsuccessful.
  11|                   § 134-5 Emergency demolition.
  12|                    Nothing in this bylaw shall restrict the Building Commissioner or the Fire Chief from ordering the
  13|                   demolition of a significant building determined to present a clear and present danger to the safety of the
  14|                   public, which only demolition can prevent. The commission shall be promptly notified of the decision.
